## I / 一

===Original===
Major Amberson had "made a fortune" in 1873, when other people were losing fortunes, and the magnificence of the Ambersons began then. Magnificence, like the size of a fortune, is always comparative, as even Magnificent Lorenzo may now perceive, if he has happened to haunt New York in 1916; and the Ambersons were magnificent in their day and place. Their splendour lasted throughout all the years that saw their Midland town spread and darken into a city, but reached its topmost during the period when every prosperous family with children kept a Newfoundland dog.

In that town, in those days, all the women who wore silk or velvet knew all the other women who wore silk or velvet, and when there was a new purchase of sealskin, sick people were got to windows to see it go by. Trotters were out, in the winter afternoons, racing light sleighs on National Avenue and Tennessee Street; everybody recognized both the trotters and the drivers; and again knew them as well on summer evenings, when slim buggies whizzed by in renewals of the snow-time rivalry. For that matter, everybody knew everybody else's family horse-and-carriage, could identify such a silhouette half a mile down the street, and thereby was sure who was going to market, or to a reception, or coming home from office or store to noon dinner or evening supper.

During the earlier years of this period, elegance of personal appearance was believed to rest more upon the texture of garments than upon their shaping. A silk dress needed no remodelling when it was a year or so old; it remained distinguished by merely remaining silk. Old men and governors wore broadcloth; "full dress" was broadcloth with "doeskin" trousers; and there were seen men of all ages to whom a hat meant only that rigid, tall silk thing known to impudence as a "stovepipe." In town and country these men would wear no other hat, and, without self-consciousness, they went rowing in such hats.

===Chinese===
安伯森少校（Major Amberson）在一八七三年“发了一笔财”——那年头，别人正纷纷破财——安伯森家的显赫便自那时肇始。显赫，同财富的规模一样，永远是比较而言的；这一点，就连“豪华者”洛伦佐（Lorenzo the Magnificent），倘若他的幽灵碰巧在一九一六年的纽约出没，想必如今也能领教；而安伯森家在他们那个时代、他们那方地面上，确乎是显赫的。他们的风光，贯穿了目睹米德兰镇（Midland town）蔓延铺展、日渐黢暗、长成一座城市的全部岁月；而极盛，则落在那种凡有子女的殷实人家都养一条纽芬兰犬的时期。

在那座城里，在那些年月，凡穿绸着绒的太太都识得其余穿绸着绒的太太；每逢谁家新置一袭海豹皮，连病人都会被扶到窗口，看它招摇而过。冬日午后，快步马纷纷出厩，拉着轻便雪橇在国民大道（National Avenue）和田纳西街（Tennessee Street）上竞逐；无论快步马还是赶车人，人人一眼便识；夏日傍晚，细腰单马轻便马车嗖嗖驶过，重演雪季的竞逐，人们照样识得它们。说实在的，谁家自用的马和马车，人人也都了然于心——隔着半英里长的街面就认得出那副剪影，由此便断定：这是上菜市去了，还是赴招待会去了，或是下了写字楼、铺子，回家来吃晌午饭、夜饭了。

在那个时期的早年，人们相信仪表的体面，更多系于衣料的质地，而非剪裁的样式。一条绸裙放上一年上下，无须改制；它只要仍旧是绸，便仍旧体面。老人和州长们都穿宽幅呢；“全套礼服”便是宽幅呢上衣配“鹿皮呢”裤；而各般年纪的男人都见得到，对他们来说，帽子只有一样东西——那种又硬又高的丝质家伙，轻狂嘴滑之徒管它叫“烟囱管”。城里乡间，这些人别无他帽，而且毫无赧颜，戴着这样的帽子去划船。

===Original===
Shifting fashions of shape replaced aristocracy of texture: dressmakers, shoemakers, hatmakers, and tailors, increasing in cunning and in power, found means to make new clothes old. The long contagion of the "Derby" hat arrived: one season the crown of this hat would be a bucket; the next it would be a spoon. Every house still kept its bootjack, but high-topped boots gave way to shoes and "congress gaiters"; and these were played through fashions that shaped them now with toes like box-ends and now with toes like the prows of racing shells.

Trousers with a crease were considered plebeian; the crease proved that the garment had lain upon a shelf, and hence was "ready-made"; these betraying trousers were called "hand-me-downs," in allusion to the shelf. In the early eighties, while bangs and bustles were having their way with women, that variation of dandy known as the "dude" was invented: he wore trousers as tight as stockings, dagger-pointed shoes, a spoon "Derby," a single-breasted coat called a "Chesterfield," with short flaring skirts, a torturing cylindrical collar, laundered to a polish and three inches high, while his other neckgear might be a heavy, puffed cravat or a tiny bow fit for a doll's braids. With evening dress he wore a tan overcoat so short that his black coattails hung visible, five inches below the overcoat; but after a season or two he lengthened his overcoat till it touched his heels, and he passed out of his tight trousers into trousers like great bags. Then, presently, he was seen no more, though the word that had been coined for him remained in the vocabularies of the impertinent.

It was a hairier day than this. Beards were to the wearers' fancy, and things as strange as the Kaiserliche boar-tusk moustache were commonplace. "Sideburns" found nourishment upon childlike profiles; great Dundreary whiskers blew like tippets over young shoulders; moustaches were trained as lambrequins over forgotten mouths; and it was possible for a Senator of the United States to wear a mist of white whisker upon his throat only, not a newspaper in the land finding the ornament distinguished enough to warrant a lampoon. Surely no more is needed to prove that so short a time ago we were living in another age!

===Chinese===
后来，款式的新潮取代了质地的门第：裁缝、鞋匠、制帽匠、成衣师傅，手腕与势力与日俱增，自有办法叫新衣裳过时。“德比帽”（Derby，即圆顶礼帽）的漫长时疫蔓延开来：这一季帽顶像只木桶，下一季又像把汤匙。家家照旧备着脱靴器，可高筒靴让位给了皮鞋和“国会式”紧口鞋；这两样又被轮番翻新的时尚摆弄个遍，鞋头一时方如箱角，一时尖似赛艇的艏柱。

带裤线的裤子被视作小家子气：有裤线便证明那裤子在搁板上搁过，是“现成货”；这种露馅的裤子便得了“递下来的旧衣”（hand-me-downs）的名号，影射的正是那块搁架。八十年代初，刘海和裙撑正在女人身上大行其道，人称“花花公子”（dude）的纨绔变种应运而生：他穿紧似袜筒的裤子、匕首尖似的鞋、汤匙顶的“德比帽”，一件单排扣、短摆外张、名唤“切斯特菲尔德”的外套；领子是折磨人的圆筒领，浆洗得锃亮，足有三英寸高；至于领子上别的东西，要么是一条鼓鼓囊囊的厚领巾，要么是一枚小得能配娃娃发辫的蝴蝶结。赴晚宴时，他披一件黄褐色大衣，短得黑燕尾服的尾巴露在外面，比大衣下摆足足长出五英寸；可过上一两季，他把大衣放长到脚跟，人又从紧腿裤挪进了大口袋似的裤腿。随后不久，此人便绝了迹，只是那个为他造出来的词，还留在出言不逊者的词典里。

那是个比眼下毛发更兴旺的时代。胡子全凭蓄者的兴致，连德皇那种野猪獠牙式的髭须，也属寻常光景。“连鬓须”在孩童般的侧脸上寻着养分；邓德里式的大颊须像皮围脖一般，在年轻人肩头飘拂；髭须被修养成垂檐帷幔，罩着一张张被遗忘的嘴；合众国参议员也有只在喉头留一片白须如雾的，而全国竟没有一家报纸觉得这装饰值得作一篇讽文。单凭这一件，便足以证明：不过这么短的岁月之前，我们生活的还是另一个时代！

===Original===
… At the beginning of the Ambersons' great period most of the houses of the Midland town were of a pleasant architecture. They lacked style, but also lacked pretentiousness, and whatever does not pretend at all has style enough. They stood in commodious yards, well shaded by leftover forest trees, elm and walnut and beech, with here and there a line of tall sycamores where the land had been made by filling bayous from the creek. The house of a "prominent resident," facing Military Square, or National Avenue, or Tennessee Street, was built of brick upon a stone foundation, or of wood upon a brick foundation. Usually it had a "front porch" and a "back porch"; often a "side porch," too. There was a "front hall"; there was a "side hall"; and sometimes a "back hall." From the "front hall" opened three rooms, the "parlour," the "sitting room," and the "library"; and the library could show warrant to its title—for some reason these people bought books. Commonly, the family sat more in the library than in the "sitting room," while callers, when they came formally, were kept to the "parlour," a place of formidable polish and discomfort. The upholstery of the library furniture was a little shabby; but the hostile chairs and sofa of the "parlour" always looked new. For all the wear and tear they got they should have lasted a thousand years.

Upstairs were the bedrooms; "mother-and-father's room" the largest; a smaller room for one or two sons, another for one or two daughters; each of these rooms containing a double bed, a "washstand," a "bureau," a wardrobe, a little table, a rocking-chair, and often a chair or two that had been slightly damaged downstairs, but not enough to justify either the expense of repair or decisive abandonment in the attic. And there was always a "spare-room," for visitors (where the sewing-machine usually was kept), and during the seventies there developed an appreciation of the necessity for a bathroom. Therefore the architects placed bathrooms in the new houses, and the older houses tore out a cupboard or two, set up a boiler beside the kitchen stove, and sought a new godliness, each with its own bathroom. The great American plumber joke, that many-branched evergreen, was planted at this time.

At the rear of the house, upstairs was a bleak little chamber, called "the girl's room," and in the stable there was another bedroom, adjoining the hayloft, and called "the hired man's room." House and stable cost seven or eight thousand dollars to build, and people with that much money to invest in such comforts were classified as the Rich. They paid the inhabitant of "the girl's room" two dollars a week, and, in the latter part of this period, two dollars and a half, and finally three dollars a week. She was Irish, ordinarily, or German or it might be Scandinavian, but never native to the land unless she happened to be a person of colour. The man or youth who lived in the stable had like wages, and sometimes he, too, was lately a steerage voyager, but much oftener he was coloured.

===Chinese===
……安伯森家的鼎盛期伊始，米德兰镇多数房屋的式样都还讨人喜欢。它们说不上风格，却也谈不上做作，而凡是全然不做作的，风格也就尽够了。房屋坐落在宽敞的院落里，幸存下来的林间老树投下浓荫——榆树、胡桃树、山毛榉；凡是从溪湾填出来的地界，这里那里总有一排高大的美国梧桐。哪位“知名住户”的宅子，面朝军事广场（Military Square）、国民大道或田纳西街，多是石基上砌砖，或砖基上立木。通常有一道“前廊”、一道“后廊”，往往还添一道“侧廊”。屋里有“前厅”，有“侧厅”，有时还有“后厅”。由“前厅”通着三个房间：“客厅”、“起居室”和“图书室”；图书室倒名实相符——不知何故，这些人是买书的。一家人平日坐在图书室里的光景，多过坐在“起居室”；而客人一旦登门，便被安置在“客厅”里——那是一处亮得咄咄逼人、坐得浑身不自在的地方。图书室的家具蒙面已见陈旧，“客厅”里那些充满敌意的座椅和沙发却永远簇新。论它们经受的那点磨损，本该撑上一千年。

楼上全是卧室；“爸妈房”最大；儿子一两个住一小间，女儿一两个另住一间；每间屋里都有双人床一张、“洗脸架”一个、五斗柜一只、衣柜一口、小桌一张、摇椅一把，往往还有一两把在楼下略受损伤的椅子——损伤得既不值得花钱修理，又没到干脆发配阁楼的地步。另外总有一间“客房”，留给客人（缝纫机通常就搁在那儿）；到了七十年代，人们渐渐领悟到浴室的必要。于是建筑师把浴室安进新盖的房子，老房子则拆掉一两个壁橱，在灶边架起锅炉，各自带着一间浴室，追求一种新的圣洁。美国那则伟大的水管工笑话——那株枝杈横生的常青树——就是这时栽下的。

屋后楼上是间冷清的小屋，人称“丫头的房间”；马厩里另有卧室，紧挨着草料棚，人称“雇工的房间”。房子带马厩，造价七八千美元；肯把这样一笔钱投在这类舒适上的，便归入富人之列。他们付给“丫头的房间”住客的工钱是每周两美元；到这个时期的后段，涨作两块半，末了涨到三块。她多半是爱尔兰人，或是德国人，兴许还是斯堪的纳维亚人，但绝不会是本地人——除非碰巧是有色人种。住在马厩里的男人或小伙，工钱相仿；有时他也是新近坐统舱漂洋而来的移民，但更常见的，他是有色人种。

===Original===
After sunrise, on pleasant mornings, the alleys behind the stables were gay; laughter and shouting went up and down their dusty lengths, with a lively accompaniment of currycombs knocking against back fences and stable walls, for the darkies loved to curry their horses in the alley. Darkies always prefer to gossip in shouts instead of whispers; and they feel that profanity, unless it be vociferous, is almost worthless. Horrible phrases were caught by early rising children and carried to older people for definition, sometimes at inopportune moments; while less investigative children would often merely repeat the phrases in some subsequent flurry of agitation, and yet bring about consequences so emphatic as to be recalled with ease in middle life.

… They have passed, those darky hired-men of the Midland town; and the introspective horses they curried and brushed and whacked and amiably cursed—those good old horses switch their tails at flies no more. For all their seeming permanence they might as well have been buffaloes—or the buffalo lap robes that grew bald in patches and used to slide from the careless drivers' knees and hang unconcerned, halfway to the ground. The stables have been transformed into other likenesses, or swept away, like the woodsheds where were kept the stove-wood and kindling that the "girl" and the "hired-man" always quarrelled over: who should fetch it. Horse and stable and woodshed, and the whole tribe of the "hired-man," all are gone. They went quickly, yet so silently that we whom they served have not yet really noticed that they are vanished.

… So with other vanishings. There were the little bunty streetcars on the long, single track that went its troubled way among the cobblestones. At the rear door of the car there was no platform, but a step where passengers clung in wet clumps when the weather was bad and the car crowded. The patrons—if not too absentminded—put their fares into a slot; and no conductor paced the heaving floor, but the driver would rap remindingly with his elbow upon the glass of the door to his little open platform if the nickels and the passengers did not appear to coincide in number. A lone mule drew the car, and sometimes drew it off the track, when the passengers would get out and push it on again. They really owed it courtesies like this, for the car was genially accommodating: a lady could whistle to it from an upstairs window, and the car would halt at once and wait for her while she shut the window, put on her hat and cloak, went downstairs, found an umbrella, told the "girl" what to have for dinner, and came forth from the house.

===Chinese===
日出之后，晴朗的早晨，马厩后头的小巷里一片欢腾；笑语和吆喝沿着尘土飞扬的巷子此起彼伏，还伴着马梳敲打后栅栏与厩壁的清脆节拍，因为黑人们喜欢在巷子里给马梳刷。黑人闲话，向来宁可扯着嗓子嚷，不肯交头接耳；他们还觉得，咒骂的粗话若不吼得山响，几乎等于白说。早起的孩子会捉到几句可怕的字眼，拿去请大人给下定义，有时挑的时机很不合适；而不那么好究根问底的孩子，也往往只消日后某阵激动中把这些字眼照本宣科一遍，便照样闹出响亮的后果，到中年回想起来，仍历历在目。

……他们都已消逝，米德兰镇那些黑人雇工；还有他们梳刷过、擦拭过、鞭打过又亲亲热热咒骂过的那些爱沉思的马——那些好性子的老马，再不会甩着尾巴驱赶苍蝇了。它们看似天长地久，实则无异于野牛——或者无异于野牛皮的车毯：那毯子一块一块地秃，常常从马夫大意的膝头滑下去，满不在乎地垂到半地。马厩改作了别的模样，或者一扫而空，柴棚也一样——从前柴棚里堆着灶柴和引火柴，“丫头”和“雇工”总为谁该去搬而拌嘴。马、马厩、柴棚，连同“雇工”的整个族群，全都不在了。他们走得很快，却悄无声息，以至于受他们服侍的我们，还没真正察觉他们已经消失。

……消逝的还有别的。有那种圆头短脑的小电车，行驶在单根长轨上，在鹅卵石路面上颠颠簸簸、艰难度日。车尾门没有平台，只有一级踏板；遇上坏天气、车又拥挤时，乘客便湿淋淋地扎成一簇，攀在上面。乘客们——只要不太健忘——把车费投进投币口；起伏晃动的车地板上没有查票员踱来踱去，只是投进去的镍币同上车的人数若显得对不上号，司机便会用肘部敲敲通往他那方寸露天平台的门玻璃，提个醒儿。拉车的是一匹孤零零的骡子，有时会把车拉出轨，乘客便下来，把车推回轨道，再各自上车。这份礼数本是他们欠这辆车的，因为它和蔼可亲、善解人意：一位太太在楼上窗口朝它吹声口哨，车便应声停下等她——等她关上窗，戴好帽子披风，下楼，寻着雨伞，吩咐“丫头”晚饭做什么，然后走出家门。

===Original===
The previous passengers made little objection to such gallantry on the part of the car: they were wont to expect as much for themselves on like occasion. In good weather the mule pulled the car a mile in a little less than twenty minutes, unless the stops were too long; but when the trolley-car came, doing its mile in five minutes and better, it would wait for nobody. Nor could its passengers have endured such a thing, because the faster they were carried the less time they had to spare! In the days before deathly contrivances hustled them through their lives, and when they had no telephones—another ancient vacancy profoundly responsible for leisure—they had time for everything: time to think, to talk, time to read, time to wait for a lady!

They even had time to dance "square dances," quadrilles, and "lancers"; they also danced the "racquette," and schottisches and polkas, and such whims as the "Portland Fancy." They pushed back the sliding doors between the "parlour" and the "sitting room," tacked down crash over the carpets, hired a few palms in green tubs, stationed three or four Italian musicians under the stairway in the "front hall"—and had great nights!

But these people were gayest on New Year's Day; they made it a true festival—something no longer known. The women gathered to "assist" the hostesses who kept "Open House"; and the carefree men, dandified and perfumed, went about in sleighs, or in carriages and ponderous "hacks," going from Open House to Open House, leaving fantastic cards in fancy baskets as they entered each doorway, and emerging a little later, more carefree than ever, if the punch had been to their liking. It always was, and, as the afternoon wore on, pedestrians saw great gesturing and waving of skintight lemon gloves, while ruinous fragments of song were dropped behind as the carriages rolled up and down the streets.

===Chinese===
车上先前的乘客，对这辆车的殷勤并不怎么见怪：换了他们在类似场合，也指望得到同等的礼遇。天气好时，骡子拉着车跑一英里，用时略不到二十分钟——除非停站太久；后来电车来了，一英里五分钟还打不住，它可谁也不等。它的乘客也受不了这种事，因为载得越快，他们腾得出的时间就越少！在那些夺命的机器驱赶着人奔完一生之前的日子里，他们也没有电话——另一种古老的空缺，对闲暇居功至伟——他们样样都有的是时间：有时间想，有时间谈，有时间读书，有时间等一位太太！

他们甚至还有时间跳舞：跳“方块舞”、四对舞和“兰斯舞”；也跳“拉凯特舞”、肖蒂什舞和波尔卡，还有“波特兰花式”这类兴之所至的名目。他们把“客厅”和“起居室”之间的推拉门推开，在地毯上钉上一层粗麻布，租来几株绿木桶栽的棕榈，在“前厅”楼梯底下安顿下三四个意大利乐师——于是便有了盛大的良宵！

可这些人最快活的日子是元旦；他们把元旦过成了真正的节庆——这样的节庆如今已无人知晓了。太太小姐们聚拢来“协助”那些“开放门户”待客的女主人；无忧无虑的男人们，衣冠楚楚、香气袭人，乘着雪橇，或是四轮马车和笨重的“出租马车”，从一处开放门户逛到另一处，每进一家门，便把花哨的名片投进精致的篮子里，过不多久再露面时，愈发无忧无虑——只要潘趣酒合他们的口味。酒向来是合口味的；午后渐深，行人便看见紧箍在手的柠檬黄手套挥手比划个不停，一支支歌的残骸断句，随着马车在街上滚来滚去，一路丢在身后。

===Original===
"Keeping Open House" was a merry custom; it has gone, like the all-day picnic in the woods, and like that prettiest of all vanished customs, the serenade. When a lively girl visited the town she did not long go unserenaded, though a visitor was not indeed needed to excuse a serenade. Of a summer night, young men would bring an orchestra under a pretty girl's window—or, it might be, her father's, or that of an ailing maiden aunt—and flute, harp, fiddle, cello, cornet, and bass viol would presently release to the dulcet stars such melodies as sing through "You'll Remember Me," "I Dreamt That I Dwelt in Marble Halls," "Silver Threads Among the Gold," "Kathleen Mavourneen," or "The Soldier's Farewell."

They had other music to offer, too, for these were the happy days of *Olivette* and *The Macotte* and *The Chimes of Normandy* and *Giroflé-Girofla* and *Fra Diavola*. Better than that, these were the days of *Pinafore* and *The Pirates of Penzance* and of *Patience*. This last was needed in the Midland town, as elsewhere, for the "aesthetic movement" had reached thus far from London, and terrible things were being done to honest old furniture. Maidens sawed what-nots in two, and gilded the remains. They took the rockers from rocking-chairs and gilded the inadequate legs; they gilded the easels that supported the crayon portraits of their deceased uncles. In the new spirit of art they sold old clocks for new, and threw wax flowers and wax fruit, and the protecting glass domes, out upon the trash-heap. They filled vases with peacock feathers, or cattails, or sumac, or sunflowers, and set the vases upon mantelpieces and marble-topped tables. They embroidered daisies (which they called "marguerites") and sunflowers and sumac and cattails and owls and peacock feathers upon plush screens and upon heavy cushions, then strewed these cushions upon floors where fathers fell over them in the dark. In the teeth of sinful oratory, the daughters went on embroidering: they embroidered daisies and sunflowers and sumac and cattails and owls and peacock feathers upon "throws" which they had the courage to drape upon horsehair sofas; they painted owls and daisies and sunflowers and sumac and cattails and peacock feathers upon tambourines. They hung Chinese umbrellas of paper to the chandeliers; they nailed paper fans to the walls. They "studied" painting on china, these girls; they sang Tosti's new songs; they sometimes still practiced the old, genteel habit of lady-fainting, and were most charming of all when they drove forth, three or four in a basket phaeton, on a spring morning.

Croquet and the mildest archery ever known were the sports of people still young and active enough for so much exertion; middle-age played euchre. There was a theatre, next door to the Amberson Hotel, and when Edwin Booth came for a night, everybody who could afford to buy a ticket was there, and all the "hacks" in town were hired. *The Black Crook* also filled the theatre, but the audience then was almost entirely of men who looked uneasy as they left for home when the final curtain fell upon the shocking girls dressed as fairies. But the theatre did not often do so well; the people of the town were still too thrifty.

===Chinese===
“开放门户”是个快活的习俗；它去了，同林间的全日野餐一道去了，也同一切消逝的习俗里顶风雅的那一样——窗下小夜曲——一道去了。活泼的姑娘来城里做客，要不了多久，窗下必有小夜曲伺候；其实也并不真需要一位客人来给小夜曲作由头。夏夜里，小伙子们会把一支小乐队带到漂亮姑娘的窗下——兴许是她父亲的窗下，或是哪位多病的未嫁姑母的窗下——长笛、竖琴、小提琴、大提琴、短号和低音提琴，随即向着柔美的星星奏出这样的曲调：《你仍会记得我》《我梦见自己住在大理石厅堂》《金丝银发》《凯瑟琳，我亲爱的》，或是《士兵的告别》。

他们另有一路音乐可献，因为那是《奥丽维特》《玛科特》《诺曼底的钟声》《吉罗弗莱-吉罗弗拉》和《魔鬼兄弟》的快乐年头。更妙的是，那还是《皮纳福号军舰》《彭赞斯的海盗》和《忍耐》的年代。最后这出戏，米德兰镇——同别处一样——正当其需，因为“唯美运动”已从伦敦一路蔓延到此，正派的老家具正惨遭荼毒。少女们把多宝架锯成两截，给残骸镀上金；她们拆掉摇椅的摇板，给那不配独站的椅腿镀金；她们给画架镀金，架上支着已故叔伯们的炭笔画像。凭着这股新的艺术精神，她们卖旧钟换新钟，把蜡花、蜡果连同护着它们的玻璃罩，统统扫上垃圾堆。她们在花瓶里插上孔雀翎、香蒲、盐肤木或是向日葵，把花瓶摆上壁炉台和大理石面小桌。她们在长绒绣屏和沉甸甸的靠垫上绣雏菊（她们管它叫“玛格丽特”）、绣向日葵、绣盐肤木、绣香蒲、绣猫头鹰、绣孔雀翎，再把这批靠垫丢在地板上，害得父亲们在黑暗里绊倒在上面。任凭训诫滔滔、斥其有罪，女儿们照绣不误：她们在“盖毯”上绣雏菊、向日葵、盐肤木、香蒲、猫头鹰和孔雀翎，还有胆量把它搭上马鬃沙发；她们在铃鼓上画猫头鹰、雏菊、向日葵、盐肤木、香蒲和孔雀翎。她们把中国纸伞挂上枝形吊灯；把纸扇钉上墙壁。这些姑娘还“钻研”瓷器彩绘；唱托斯蒂的新歌；偶尔还操练贵妇人昏厥的老派雅习；而春日上午，三四人同乘一辆篮式敞篷轻车出游时，她们最是动人。

槌球，加上有史以来最温和的射箭游戏，是那些还算年轻、还算折腾得动的人的消遣；中年人打尤克牌。城里有一家剧院，就在安伯森旅馆（Amberson Hotel）隔壁；埃德温·布思（Edwin Booth）来演一个晚上时，买得起票的人全到了场，全城的“出租马车”都被雇空。《黑钩子》上演时剧院也座无虚席，只是那晚的观众几乎清一色是男人；当大幕最后落在那些扮作仙女的惊世骇俗的姑娘身上，他们往家走时个个神色不安。不过剧院并非回回这般风光；城里人到底节俭得紧。

===Original===
They were thrifty because they were the sons or grandsons of the "early settlers," who had opened the wilderness and had reached it from the East and the South with wagons and axes and guns, but with no money at all. The pioneers were thrifty or they would have perished: they had to store away food for the winter, or goods to trade for food, and they often feared they had not stored enough—they left traces of that fear in their sons and grandsons. In the minds of most of these, indeed, their thrift was next to their religion: to save, even for the sake of saving, was their earliest lesson and discipline. No matter how prosperous they were, they could not spend money either upon "art," or upon mere luxury and entertainment, without a sense of sin.

Against so homespun a background the magnificence of the Ambersons was as conspicuous as a brass band at a funeral. Major Amberson bought two hundred acres of land at the end of National Avenue; and through this tract he built broad streets and cross-streets; paved them with cedar block, and curbed them with stone. He set up fountains, here and there, where the streets intersected, and at symmetrical intervals placed cast-iron statues, painted white, with their titles clear upon the pedestals: Minerva, Mercury, Hercules, Venus, Gladiator, Emperor Augustus, Fisher Boy, Staghound, Mastiff, Greyhound, Fawn, Antelope, Wounded Doe, and Wounded Lion. Most of the forest trees had been left to flourish still, and, at some distance, or by moonlight, the place was in truth beautiful; but the ardent citizen, loving to see his city grow, wanted neither distance nor moonlight. He had not seen Versailles, but, standing before the Fountain of Neptune in Amberson Addition, at bright noon, and quoting the favourite comparison of the local newspapers, he declared Versailles outdone. All this Art showed a profit from the start, for the lots sold well and there was something like a rush to build in the new Addition. Its main thoroughfare, an oblique continuation of National Avenue, was called Amberson Boulevard, and here, at the juncture of the new Boulevard and the Avenue, Major Amberson reserved four acres for himself, and built his new house—the Amberson Mansion, of course.

This house was the pride of the town. Faced with stone as far back as the dining-room windows, it was a house of arches and turrets and girdling stone porches: it had the first porte-cochère seen in that town. There was a central "front hall" with a great black walnut stairway, and open to a green glass skylight called the "dome," three stories above the ground floor. A ballroom occupied most of the third story; and at one end of it was a carved walnut gallery for the musicians. Citizens told strangers that the cost of all this black walnut and woodcarving was sixty thousand dollars. "Sixty thousand dollars for the woodwork alone! Yes, sir, and hardwood floors all over the house! Turkish rugs and no carpets at all, except a Brussels carpet in the front parlour—I hear they call it the 'reception-room.' Hot and cold water upstairs and down, and stationary washstands in every last bedroom in the place! Their sideboard's built right into the house and goes all the way across one end of the dining room. It isn't walnut, it's solid mahogany! Not veneering—solid mahogany! Well, sir, I presume the President of the United States would be tickled to swap the White House for the new Amberson Mansion, if the Major'd give him the chance—but by the Almighty Dollar, you bet your sweet life the Major wouldn't!"

===Chinese===
他们节俭，因为他们是“早期移民”的儿子或孙子——那些人开发荒野，从东部和南方赶着大车、带着斧子和枪来到此地，却身无分文。拓荒者不节俭就活不成：他们必须为冬天囤下粮食，或是囤下能换粮食的货物，还常常唯恐囤得不够——这份恐惧，在他们儿子和孙子身上留下了印迹。说实在的，对这多数人而言，节俭近乎宗教：攒钱，哪怕只为攒钱而攒钱，是他们最早领受的功课与训诫。无论家业多么兴旺，他们花钱买“艺术”，或是买纯粹的奢侈品和娱乐，都免不了心生罪感。

在这样一派土布底色的背景上，安伯森家的显赫便像葬礼上的一支铜管乐队那样扎眼。安伯森少校在国民大道尽头买下两百英亩土地；在这片地界上，他修出宽阔的街道和横街，用雪松木块铺路面，用石料砌路缘。街口相交之处，他东一处西一处立起喷泉，又以对称的间隔安放铸铁雕像，漆成白色，底座上把名号凿得清清楚楚：密涅瓦、墨丘利、赫拉克勒斯、维纳斯、角斗士、奥古斯都皇帝、渔童、猎鹿犬、獒犬、灵缇、幼鹿、羚羊、受伤的母鹿、受伤的狮子。林地上的树木大半还留着一味繁茂；隔着一段距离看，或是月色之下，这地方的确美；可那位热心的市民一心要看自己的城市长大，既不要距离，也不要月光。凡尔赛他是没见过的，不过站在安伯森新城区（Amberson Addition）的尼普顿喷泉前，时值晌午，日头大亮，他引述本地报纸最爱用的那句比较，宣布凡尔赛已被盖过。这整宗艺术从开张头一天起就见盈利，因为地皮卖得红火，新区里几乎掀起了一场建房热潮。新区的干道，斜斜地接续国民大道，名为安伯森大道（Amberson Boulevard）；就在新大道与旧大道交汇处，安伯森少校给自己留下四英亩地，盖起了他的新宅——当然，安伯森公馆。

这座房子是全城的骄傲。正面直到餐厅窗户一线皆以石料镶面，整座宅子满是拱券、角楼和环宅的石廊：镇上见着的第一座带顶车马门廊（porte-cochère）便出在这里。中央的“前厅”里立着黑胡桃木大楼梯，厅身直上三层楼高，敞通一扇人称“穹顶”的绿玻璃天窗。三楼大半是个舞厅，厅堂一头有雕花胡桃木廊台，供乐师用。市民们对外乡人说，光是这些黑胡桃木和木雕活儿，就花了六万美元。“光木工就六万美元！没错，先生，满屋都是硬木地板！土耳其地毯，别的地毯一概没有，只有前客厅铺一块布鲁塞尔地毯——听说他们管那儿叫'接待室'。楼上楼下冷热水俱全，每间卧室都装固定式洗脸台，一间不落！他家餐具柜是跟房子连着盖的，横贯餐厅一头。那不是胡桃木，是整料桃花心木！不是贴面——是整料桃花心木！哼，先生，依我看，只要少校肯给机会，合众国总统乐意拿白宫换这座新的安伯森公馆都说不定——不过拿万能的美元起誓，我敢拿性命打包票，少校绝不肯换！”

===Original===
The visitor to the town was certain to receive further enlightenment, for there was one form of entertainment never omitted: he was always patriotically taken for "a little drive around our city," even if his host had to hire a hack, and the climax of the display was the Amberson Mansion. "Look at that greenhouse they've put up there in the side yard," the escort would continue. "And look at that brick stable! Most folks would think that stable plenty big enough and good enough to live in; it's got running water and four rooms upstairs for two hired men and one of 'em's family to live in. They keep one hired man loafin' in the house, and they got a married hired man out in the stable, and his wife does the washing. They got box-stalls for four horses, and they keep a coupay, and some new kinds of fancy rigs you never saw the beat of! 'Carts' they call two of 'em—way up in the air they are—too high for me! I guess they got every new kind of fancy rig in there that's been invented. And harness—well, everybody in town can tell when Ambersons are out driving after dark, by the jingle. This town never did see so much style as Ambersons are putting on, these days; and I guess it's going to be expensive, because a lot of other folks'll try to keep up with 'em. The Major's wife and the daughter's been to Europe, and my wife tells me since they got back they make tea there every afternoon about five o'clock, and drink it. Seems to me it would go against a person's stomach, just before supper like that, and anyway tea isn't fit for much—not unless you're sick or something. My wife says Ambersons don't make lettuce salad the way other people do; they don't chop it up with sugar and vinegar at all. They pour olive oil on it with their vinegar, and they have it separate—not along with the rest of the meal. And they *eat* these olives, too: green things they are, something like a hard plum, but a friend of mine told me they tasted a good deal like a bad hickory-nut. My wife says she's going to buy some; you got to eat nine and then you get to like 'em, she says. Well, I wouldn't eat nine bad hickory-nuts to get to like *them*, and I'm going to let these olives alone. Kind of a woman's dish, anyway, I suspect, but most everybody'll be makin' a stagger to worm through nine of 'em, now Ambersons brought 'em to town. Yes, sir, the rest'll eat 'em, whether they get sick or not! Looks to me like some people in this city'd be willing to go crazy if they thought that would help 'em to be as high-toned as Ambersons. Old Aleck Minafer—he's about the closest old codger we got—he come in my office the other day, and he pretty near had a stroke tellin' me about his daughter Fanny. Seems Miss Isabel Amberson's got some kind of a dog—they call it a Saint Bernard—and Fanny was bound to have one, too. Well, old Aleck told her he didn't like dogs except rat-terriers, because a rat-terrier cleans up the mice, but she kept on at him, and finally he said all right she could have one. Then, by George! she says Ambersons *bought* their dog, and you can't get one without paying for it: they cost from fifty to a hundred dollars up! Old Aleck wanted to know if I ever heard of anybody buyin' a dog before, because, of course, even a Newfoundland or a setter you can usually get somebody to give you one. He says he saw some sense in payin' a nigger a dime, or even a quarter, to *drown* a dog for you, but to pay out fifty dollars and maybe more—well, sir, he like to choked himself to death, right there in my office! Of course everybody realizes that Major Amberson is a fine business man, but what with throwin' money around for dogs, and every which and what, some think all this style's bound to break him up, if his family don't quit!"

One citizen, having thus discoursed to a visitor, came to a thoughtful pause, and then added, "Does seem pretty much like squandering, yet when you see that dog out walking with this Miss Isabel, he seems worth the money."

"What's she look like?"

"Well, sir," said the citizen, "she's not more than just about eighteen or maybe nineteen years old, and I don't know as I know just how to put it—but she's kind of a *delightful* lookin' young lady!"

===Chinese===
来访的外乡人必定得到进一步的教益，因为有一样节目是断不会省去的：主人总要出于爱国心，带他去“围着咱们市兜一小圈风”，哪怕得临时雇一辆出租马车；而这场展览的压轴戏，便是安伯森公馆。“瞧见侧院里新起的暖房没有，”带路的东道主接着说，“再瞧那座砖砌马厩！多数人会当这马厩又大又好，住人都绰绰有余：有自来水，楼上有四间屋，够两个雇工外加一家的老小住。家里另养着一个雇工白吃饭，马厩里还有一个带家眷的，他老婆管洗衣服。四个单间马栏，养四匹马；还有一辆双座小马车，外加几种你没见过比这更神气的新式时髦车驾！其中两样他们管它叫'高车'——高高在上，离地老远——我可坐不惯！依我看，凡发明出来的新式车驾，他们家里一样不缺。再说挽具——嘿，天黑以后安伯森家出门兜风，满城只听铃声就知道。这座城还从来没见过安伯森家眼下这样的排场；我料定这排场费钱，因为好些人家都要想法子跟上他们。少校夫人和小姐去过欧洲，我家那口子告诉我，打她们回来，每天下午五点来钟就烧茶、喝茶。依我说，临上晚饭前灌那一肚子茶，多伤人的胃；再说茶这东西本来就没多大用——除非你害病什么的。我家那口子说，安伯森家拌莴苣凉菜不跟别人一个路数：人家根本不拿糖和醋剁在一起，是把橄榄油连醋浇上去，而且单摆浮搁着吃——不跟一桌饭一道。橄榄他们也*吃*：青乎乎的东西，有点像没熟的李子，可我一个朋友说，味道倒很有些像坏山核桃。我家那口子说她要去买些来；她说吃上九个，你就吃出味儿来了。哼，要吃九个坏山核桃才吃得出味儿的东西，我不干，橄榄这玩意儿我就敬谢不敏了。再说多半是娘儿们的小碟，可如今安伯森家把橄榄带进了城，差不多人人都要硬着头皮，费劲巴拉地咽满九个。没错，先生，别家照吃，吃出病来也吃！照我看，这城里有些人，只要发疯能帮他们够上安伯森家那么高格调，他们愿意疯一疯。老亚历克·米纳弗（Aleck Minafer）——他是我们这儿顶抠门的老家伙——前几天上我办公室来，说起他女儿范妮（Fanny），险些当场中风。敢情安伯森家的伊莎贝尔小姐（Miss Isabel Amberson）养了条什么狗——他们管它叫圣伯纳——范妮非要也有一条不可。老亚历克跟她说，狗里头他只瞧得上捕鼠梗，因为捕鼠梗能收拾耗子；可她缠个没完，末了他松了口，说她可以养一条。后来，好家伙！她说安伯森家的狗是*买*来的，不掏钱弄不来：价钱从五十块到一百块往上！老亚历克问我，从前可曾听说过谁买狗——因为，当然了，就算是纽芬兰犬或塞特猎犬，通常总能寻摸个白送的。他说，花一毛钱、哪怕两毛五，雇个黑鬼替你把狗*淹死*，他还能想出点道理来；可要掏五十块甚至更多——嗐，先生，他当场在我办公室里，险些没把自己憋死！当然了，人人都承认安伯森少校是位生意好手；可又为狗乱撒钱，又这样那样，有人觉得，这家人要再不收着点儿，这份排场迟早把他拖垮！”

有一位市民，对外乡人发了这通议论，沉思着顿了顿，又找补一句：“瞧着是挺像挥霍，可你要是见过那条狗陪着伊莎贝尔小姐遛弯儿的模样，就觉得这钱花得值了。”

“那小姐长得什么样？”

“这个嘛，先生，”市民说，“她至多十八九岁的年纪，我也说不好该怎么讲——反正她是那种看着*实在可爱*的年轻小姐！”

## II / 二

===Original===
Another citizen said an eloquent thing about Miss Isabel Amberson's looks. This was Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster, the foremost literary authority and intellectual leader of the community—for both the daily newspapers thus described Mrs. Foster when she founded the Women's Tennyson Club; and her word upon art, letters, and the drama was accepted more as law than as opinion. Naturally, when Hazel Kirke finally reached the town, after its long triumph in larger places, many people waited to hear what Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster thought of it before they felt warranted in expressing any estimate of the play. In fact, some of them waited in the lobby of the theatre, as they came out, and formed an inquiring group about her.

"I didn't see the play," she informed them.

"What! Why, we saw you, right in the middle of the fourth row!"

"Yes," she said, smiling, "but I was sitting just behind Isabel Amberson. I couldn't look at anything except her wavy brown hair and the wonderful back of her neck."

===Chinese===
另一位市民就伊莎贝尔·安伯森（Isabel Amberson）小姐的容貌，说过一句妙语。这位市民是亨利·富兰克林·福斯特太太（Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster）——她创立妇女丁尼生俱乐部（Women's Tennyson Club）的时候，两家日报正是这样称许她的；至于艺术、文学、戏剧上的事，她的评判，人们奉之如律法，并不当作一家之言。自然，等《黑兹尔·柯克》（Hazel Kirke）在更大的城市里风光够了、终于巡演到本城时，许多人都先要听听福斯特太太的高见，才觉得自己有资格对这出戏说三道四。其实，那晚就有几个人散场时守在戏院门厅里，围着她站成一圈，等着讨教。

“我没看戏。”她告诉大家。

“什么！咦，我们明明看见你了，就坐在第四排正当中！”

“是啊，”她笑吟吟地说，“可我恰恰坐在伊莎贝尔·安伯森背后。除了她那头波浪似的棕发和那截妙不可言的颈背，我什么也没能看成。”

===Original===
The ineligible young men of the town (they were all ineligible) were unable to content themselves with the view that had so charmed Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster: they spent their time struggling to keep Miss Amberson's face turned toward them. She turned it most often, observers said, toward two: one excelling in the general struggle by his sparkle, and the other by that winning if not winsome old trait, persistence. The sparkling gentleman "led germans" with her, and sent sonnets to her with his bouquets—sonnets lacking neither music nor wit. He was generous, poor, well-dressed, and his amazing persuasiveness was one reason why he was always in debt. No one doubted that he would be able to persuade Isabel, but he unfortunately joined too merry a party one night, and, during a moonlight serenade upon the lawn before the Amberson Mansion, was easily identified from the windows as the person who stepped through the bass viol and had to be assisted to a waiting carriage. One of Miss Amberson's brothers was among the serenaders, and, when the party had dispersed, remained propped against the front door in a state of helpless liveliness; the Major going down in a dressing-gown and slippers to bring him in, and scolding mildly, while imperfectly concealing strong impulses to laughter. Miss Amberson also laughed at this brother, the next day, but for the suitor it was a different matter: she refused to see him when he called to apologize. "You seem to care a great deal about bass viols!" he wrote her. "I promise never to break another." She made no response to the note, unless it was an answer, two weeks later, when her engagement was announced. She took the persistent one, Wilbur Minafer, no breaker of bass viols or of hearts, no serenader at all.

===Chinese===
本城那些不够格的年轻求婚者（他们个个都不够格），可不满足于让福斯特太太心醉神迷的那点儿风景：他们一门心思，只求安伯森小姐把脸转过来朝着自己。据旁观的人说，这张脸转得最勤的，是朝着两个人：一个凭满身的风头在群雄逐鹿中拔了尖，另一个靠的则是那种未必悦目、却总能得分的老脾气——磨不完的韧劲。那位神采飞扬的先生，舞会上同她共领“日耳曼舞”，花束里还夹上十四行诗——音韵、才情两不缺。他慷慨、清贫、衣冠楚楚，而那手惊人的游说功夫，也正是他债台高筑的缘由之一。没有人怀疑他终有一天能把伊莎贝尔说动；只可惜一天夜里，他赴宴乐过了头，在安伯森公馆（the Amberson Mansion）前草坪的月下小夜曲上，被人从窗口一眼认了出来——正是那个一脚踩穿了低音提琴、还得让人架着送上候在旁边马车的人物。小夜曲队伍里，恰有安伯森小姐的一位兄弟；众人散尽之后，他还斜倚在前门上，乐得站都站不直；安伯森少校（the Major）穿着晨衣、趿着拖鞋下楼来把他往屋里领，嘴里轻轻数落，却怎么也掩不住一阵阵想笑的冲动。第二天，安伯森小姐也取笑了这位兄弟；可轮到那位求婚者，就是另一回事了：他登门赔罪，她拒不相见。“看来你对低音提琴的感情很不一般哪！”他写信给她。“我保证再也不踩坏第二把。”对这张字条她没有回音——除非两个星期后订婚启事登了出来，算是给了答复。她挑中的是那个磨不完的韧劲的主儿，威尔伯·米纳弗（Wilbur Minafer）：提琴不曾踩坏过一把，姑娘的心也不曾碎过一颗，小夜曲更是从来不唱。

===Original===
A few people, who always foresaw everything, claimed that they were not surprised, because though Wilbur Minafer "might not be an Apollo, as it were," he was "a steady young business man, and a good churchgoer," and Isabel Amberson was "pretty sensible—for such a showy girl." But the engagement astounded the young people, and most of their fathers and mothers, too; and as a topic it supplanted literature at the next meeting of the Women's Tennyson Club.

"Wilbur *Minafer*!" a member cried, her inflection seeming to imply that Wilbur's crime was explained by his surname. "Wilbur *Minafer*! It's the queerest thing I ever heard! To think of her taking Wilbur Minafer, just because a man *any* woman would like a thousand times better was a little wild one night at a serenade!"

"No," said Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster. "It isn't that. It isn't even because she's afraid he'd be a dissipated husband and she wants to be safe. It isn't because she's religious or hates wildness; it isn't even because she hates wildness in *him*."

===Chinese===
少数几个凡事都料得到的人声称毫不意外：威尔伯·米纳弗“虽说算不上什么阿波罗”，到底是“一个踏实的年轻生意人，做礼拜也勤”，而伊莎贝尔·安伯森呢，“也够明白事理的——对这么个扎眼的姑娘来说”。可这桩订婚把年轻人震住了，连他们的大多数爹妈也给震住了；在妇女丁尼生俱乐部的下一次聚会上，这个话题把文学挤下了台。

“威尔伯·‘米纳弗’！”一位会员嚷道，那腔调似乎在暗示：威尔伯的罪过，从他的姓氏里就能找出答案。“威尔伯·‘米纳弗’！我活了这么久，没听过第二件这么古怪的事！想想吧，她竟然嫁了威尔伯·米纳弗——就因为随便‘哪个’女人都会喜欢上一千倍的那个男人，在一次小夜曲上野了一个晚上！”

“不对，”亨利·富兰克林·福斯特太太说，“不是这么回事。甚至也不是因为她怕摊上个荒唐的丈夫、想图个安稳。不是因为虔诚，也不是恨荒唐；甚至也不是因为她恨的是‘他’荒唐。”

===Original===
"Well, but look how she's thrown him over for it."

"No, that wasn't her reason," said the wise Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster. "If men only knew it—and it's a good thing they don't—a woman doesn't really care much about whether a man's wild or not, if it doesn't affect herself, and Isabel Amberson doesn't care a thing!"

"Mrs. *Foster*!"

"No, she doesn't. What she minds is his making a clown of himself in her front yard! It made her think he didn't care much about *her*. She's probably mistaken, but that's what she thinks, and it's too late for her to think anything else now, because she's going to be married right away—the invitations will be out next week. It'll be a big Amberson-style thing, raw oysters floating in scooped-out blocks of ice and a band from out-of-town—champagne, showy presents; a colossal present from the Major. Then Wilbur will take Isabel on the carefulest little wedding trip he can manage, and she'll be a good wife to him, but they'll have the worst spoiled lot of children this town will ever see."

===Chinese===
“咦，可你瞧，她不就为这个把他甩了嘛。”

“不，那不是她的理由，”明白人福斯特太太说，“男人家要是能明白这个道理就好了——不明白倒是桩好事——只要碍不着女人自己，那个男人野一点还是不野一点，她其实并不怎么在乎；伊莎贝尔·安伯森更是一丁点儿也不在乎！”

“福斯特‘太太’！”

“不，她不在乎。她介意的，是他跑到她家前院里出乖露丑！这就让她认定，他并没有把她放在多重的心上。她多半是想岔了，可她认定就是这个理，如今她也没有工夫再想旁的了，因为她眼看就要办喜事——请帖下个礼拜就发。那准是安伯森家的大场面：掏空的冰坨里浮着生蚝，乐队从外埠请来——香槟、扎眼的贺礼，少校还备下一份天大的厚礼。往后，威尔伯会带伊莎贝尔去度一个他安排得出来的最最当心的小蜜月，她也会做他的贤妻；不过他们养出来的儿女，管保是本城有史以来娇纵得最不成样子的一窝。”

===Original===
"How on earth do you make *that* out, Mrs. Foster?"

"She couldn't love Wilbur, could she?" Mrs. Foster demanded, with no challengers. "Well, it will all go to her children, and she'll ruin 'em!"

The prophetess proved to be mistaken in a single detail merely: except for that, her foresight was accurate. The wedding was of Ambersonian magnificence, even to the floating oysters; and the Major's colossal present was a set of architect's designs for a house almost as elaborate and impressive as the Mansion, the house to be built in Amberson Addition by the Major. The orchestra was certainly not that local one which had suffered the loss of a bass viol; the musicians came, according to the prophecy and next morning's paper, from afar; and at midnight the bride was still being toasted in champagne, though she had departed upon her wedding journey at ten. Four days later the pair had returned to town, which promptness seemed fairly to demonstrate that Wilbur had indeed taken Isabel upon the carefulest little trip he could manage. According to every report, she was from the start "a good wife to him," but here in a final detail the prophecy proved inaccurate. Wilbur and Isabel did not have children; they had only one.

===Chinese===
“福斯特太太，您‘那’个结论究竟是怎么推出来的？”

“她总不见得爱威尔伯，对吧？”福斯特太太逼问了一句。没有人应战。“那就得了：她那份心，往后全会倾到儿女身上，她非把他们惯坏了不可！”

这位女先知的预言，只在一个细节上走了空；除此之外，料事如神。婚礼极尽安伯森家的奢华，连浮着的生蚝都应了验；少校的天大厚礼，是一套建筑师绘制的宅邸图纸——那房子几乎同安伯森大宅一样精致气派，将由少校造在安伯森新城区（Amberson Addition）里。乐队当然不是本地那支折了低音提琴的班子；正如预言和第二天早晨的报纸所说，乐师们远道而来；到了半夜，新娘还在领受香槟祝酒，尽管她十点钟就已经动身，踏上了新婚的旅途。四天之后，小两口便回了城——这份利落似乎足以证明，威尔伯果真带着伊莎贝尔，度了一个他安排得出来的最最当心的小蜜月。人人都说她从一开始就是“他的贤妻”；只是在最后这个细节上，预言失了准头：威尔伯和伊莎贝尔不是儿女成群——他们只有一个。

===Original===
"Only one," Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster admitted. "But I'd like to know if he isn't spoiled enough for a whole carload!"

Again she found none to challenge her.

At the age of nine, George Amberson Minafer, the Major's one grandchild, was a princely terror, dreaded not only in Amberson Addition but in many other quarters through which he galloped on his white pony. "By golly, I guess you think you own this town!" an embittered labourer complained, one day, as Georgie rode the pony straight through a pile of sand the man was sieving. "I will when I grow up," the undisturbed child replied. "I guess my grandpa owns it now, you bet!" And the baffled workman, having no means to controvert what seemed a mere exaggeration of the facts, could only mutter, "Oh, pull down your vest!"

===Chinese===
“是只有一个，”亨利·富兰克林·福斯特太太承认，“可我倒想请教：这一个小家伙的娇惯劲儿，难道还抵不上一整车的娃！”

这一回，依旧无人应战。

九岁上，乔治·安伯森·米纳弗（George Amberson Minafer）——少校唯一的外孙——已经是个小霸王，威风得像位王爷；不但在安伯森新城区人人谈之色变，他骑着小白马驰骋过的其他许多街区，也无不闻风丧胆。有一天，小乔治（Georgie）骑着马，径直从一个筛沙工人筛好的沙堆上蹚了过去，那工人满腔怨气地抱怨道：“好啊你，我看你八成觉得这座城是你家的吧！”“等我长大了就是。”孩子神色不动。“我外公如今就占着它呢，这还用说！”那工人被噎得没了下文，又驳不倒这句听上去不过是把事实夸了一夸的话，只好嘟囔一句：“哼，把你的背心拉拉好！”

===Original===
"Don't haf to! Doctor says it ain't healthy!" the boy returned promptly. "But I'll tell you what I'll do: I'll pull down my vest if you'll wipe off your chin!"

This was stock and stencil: the accustomed argot of street badinage of the period; and in such matters Georgie was an expert. He had no vest to pull down; the incongruous fact was that a fringed sash girdled the juncture of his velvet blouse and breeches, for the Fauntleroy period had set in, and Georgie's mother had so poor an eye for appropriate things, where Georgie was concerned, that she dressed him according to the doctrine of that school in boy decoration. Not only did he wear a silk sash, and silk stockings, and a broad lace collar, with his little black velvet suit: he had long brown curls, and often came home with burrs in them.

Except upon the surface (which was not his own work, but his mother's) Georgie bore no vivid resemblance to the fabulous little Cedric. The storied boy's famous "Lean on *me*, grandfather," would have been difficult to imagine upon the lips of Georgie. A month after his ninth birthday anniversary, when the Major gave him his pony, he had already become acquainted with the toughest boys in various distant parts of the town, and had convinced them that the toughness of a rich little boy with long curls might be considered in many respects superior to their own. He fought them, learning how to go berserk at a certain point in a fight, bursting into tears of anger, reaching for rocks, uttering wailed threats of murder and attempting to fulfil them. Fights often led to intimacies, and he acquired the art of saying things more exciting than "Don't haf to!" and "Doctor says it ain't healthy!" Thus, on a summer afternoon, a strange boy, sitting bored upon the gatepost of the Reverend Malloch Smith, beheld George Amberson Minafer rapidly approaching on his white pony, and was impelled by bitterness to shout: "Shoot the ole jackass! Look at the girly curls! Say, bub, where'd you steal your mother's ole sash!"

===Chinese===
“不用拉！大夫说拉下来不健康！”孩子立刻顶了回去。“不过我可以跟你做桩买卖：你肯擦净你的下巴，我就拉下我的背心！”

这一套是现成的版子、刻好的模子：当年街头斗嘴的行话就是这一路；而在这门学问上，小乔治是个行家。他身上根本没有背心可拉；说来不合榫头的是，束在他丝绒上衣和短裤交界处的，是一条滚边的绸腰带——“方特罗伊”（Fauntleroy）时代已然登场，而小乔治的母亲，但凡牵涉到小乔治，眼力就差得离谱，竟照着那一派男童装扮的学说给他梳妆。这身小小的黑丝绒套装，配上绸腰带、丝袜和宽宽的蕾丝领还不算：他还留着一头棕色的长鬈发，并且常常顶着满头苍耳回家。

除去那身行头（那也不是他自己的心思，是他母亲的），小乔治同传说里那位小塞德里克（Cedric）并无活灵活现的相似之处。故事里那男孩的名言“外公，靠‘我’吧”，放在小乔治嘴上实在无从想象。九岁生日过后满一个月，少校把小马送给了他；那会儿他早已结识了城里远近各处最横的小子，而且叫他们服了气：一个留着长鬈发的阔少爷，那股横劲儿，好些地方竟在他们之上。他跟他们打架，练出了一套本事：打到一定火候便撒泼发狂，气得放声大哭，伸手抄石头，哭着喊要杀人，并且真去试着兑现。打架常常打出交情来，他又学会了说些比“不用拉！”“大夫说拉下来不健康！”更带劲的话。于是，一个夏日的午后，一个陌生的男孩正百无聊赖地坐在马洛奇·史密斯牧师（the Reverend Malloch Smith）家的门柱上，望见乔治·安伯森·米纳弗骑着小白马飞驰而来，一股酸气顶了上来，大声嚷道：“打死你个老驴！瞧那一头娘儿们的鬈毛！喂，小子，你偷你妈那条旧腰带，是从哪儿偷的！”

===Original===
"Your sister stole it for me!" Georgie instantly replied, checking the pony. "She stole it off our clo'es-line an' gave it to me."

"You go get your hair cut!" said the stranger hotly. "Yah! I haven't got any sister!"

"I know you haven't at home," Georgie responded. "I mean the one that's in jail."

"I dare you to get down off that pony!"

Georgie jumped to the ground, and the other boy descended from the Reverend Mr. Smith's gatepost—but he descended inside the gate. "I dare you outside that gate," said Georgie.

===Chinese===
“是你姐姐替我偷的！”小乔治勒住马，张口就回。“她打咱们家晾衣绳上偷下来给我的。”

“你给我去把头发铰了！”那陌生人火冒三丈。“呸！我压根儿没有姐姐！”

“你家里没有，这个我知道，”小乔治答道，“我说的是蹲大狱的那一个。”

“有本事你从马背上下来！”

小乔治一跃跳到地上，那孩子也从史密斯牧师家的门柱上下来了——不过他是落在门里头的。“有本事你出这道门。”小乔治说。

===Original===
"Yah! I dare you halfway here. I dare you—"

But these were luckless challenges, for Georgie immediately vaulted the fence—and four minutes later Mrs. Malloch Smith, hearing strange noises, looked forth from a window; then screamed, and dashed for the pastor's study. Mr. Malloch Smith, that grim-bearded Methodist, came to the front yard and found his visiting nephew being rapidly prepared by Master Minafer to serve as a principal figure in a pageant of massacre. It was with great physical difficulty that Mr. Smith managed to give his nephew a chance to escape into the house, for Georgie was hard and quick, and, in such matters, remarkably intense; but the minister, after a grotesque tussle, got him separated from his opponent, and shook him.

"You stop that, you!" Georgie cried fiercely; and wrenched himself away. "I guess you don't know who I am!"

"Yes, I do know!" the angered Mr. Smith retorted. "I know who you are, and you're a disgrace to your mother! Your mother ought to be ashamed of herself to allow—"

"Shut up about my mother bein' ashamed of herself!"

===Chinese===
“呸！有本事你走到半道上。有本事你——”

可这些叫阵偏偏不吉利：小乔治立刻翻过了栅栏——四分钟后，马洛奇·史密斯太太听见院子里怪声连天，从窗口一张望，随即尖叫一声，直奔牧师的书房而去。马洛奇·史密斯先生——蓄着一脸凛然络腮胡的卫理公会牧师——来到前院，只见他前来做客的小外甥，正被米纳弗少爷紧锣密鼓地“操办”着，要在一场大屠杀的活报剧里出演要角。史密斯先生费了好大的气力，才总算给外甥争出一个逃进屋去的机会，因为小乔治出手又狠又快，而且在这样的事情上格外来劲；牧师经过一场狼狈不堪的扭打，总算把两个孩子拉开了，随即把他摇撼了几下。

“你给我住手，你这个家伙！”小乔治凶狠狠地喊，一面挣脱身子。“我看你不知道我是谁！”

“我当然知道！”史密斯先生动了怒，回敬道。“我知道你是谁；你是叫你母亲丢脸的东西！你母亲容你这样，她自己就该害臊——”

“不许再说害臊不害臊我妈的事！”

===Original===
Mr. Smith, exasperated, was unable to close the dialogue with dignity. "She ought to be ashamed," he repeated. "A woman that lets a bad boy like you—"

But Georgie had reached his pony and mounted. Before setting off at his accustomed gallop, he paused to interrupt the Reverend Malloch Smith again. "You pull down your vest, you ole Billygoat, you!" he shouted, distinctly. "Pull down your vest, wipe off your chin—an' go to hell!"

Such precocity is less unusual, even in children of the Rich, than most grown people imagine. However, it was a new experience for the Reverend Malloch Smith, and left him in a state of excitement. He at once wrote a note to Georgie's mother, describing the crime according to his nephew's testimony; and the note reached Mrs. Minafer before Georgie did. When he got home she read it to him sorrowfully.

===Chinese===
史密斯先生怒不可遏，这场对话想收个体面也收不成了。“她就是该害臊，”他重复道，“一个女人，放任你这么个坏小子——”

可小乔治已经跑到小马跟前，翻身骑了上去。照例起跑疾驰之前，他停了一下，又把马洛奇·史密斯牧师的话头打断了一回。“把你的背心拉拉好，你这个老山羊，说的就是你！”他一字一顿地嚷道。“拉下背心，擦净下巴——下地狱去吧！”

这样的早熟，即便在富家子弟里头，也不像大多数大人想象的那么稀罕。不过，对马洛奇·史密斯牧师来说，这总是破题儿第一遭，叫他激动不已。他当即给小乔治的母亲写了张字条，照他外甥的证词把这桩罪案描述了一番；字条比小乔治先到了米纳弗太太手里。他到家时，她哀伤地把信念给他听。

===Original===
> Dear Madam:
> Your son has caused a painful distress in my household. He made an unprovoked attack upon a little nephew of mine who is visiting in my household, insulted him by calling him vicious names and falsehoods, stating that ladies of his family were in jail. He then tried to make his pony kick him, and when the child, who is only eleven years old, while your son is much older and stronger, endeavoured to avoid his indignities and withdraw quietly, he pursued him into the enclosure of my property and brutally assaulted him. When I appeared upon this scene he deliberately called insulting words to me, concluding with profanity, such as "go to hell," which was heard not only by myself but by my wife and the lady who lives next door. I trust such a state of undisciplined behaviour may be remedied for the sake of the reputation for propriety, if nothing higher, of the family to which this unruly child belongs.

===Chinese===
> 敬爱的太太：
> 令郎已在我的家中造成一场令人痛心的纷扰。他无缘无故袭击了我府上正在做客的一个小外甥，用恶毒的假话辱骂他，声称他家的女眷关在牢里。随后他又驱使小马去踢那孩子；那孩子年仅十一岁，令郎却年长得多、也壮得多，本想避开羞辱、悄悄退开，他竟追进我的宅界，野蛮地把他殴打了一顿。我现身现场之后，他又存心对我口出侮辱之词，末了还加上渎神的脏话，诸如“下地狱去吧”，不但我本人听见，我的妻子和隔壁的太太也都在场听见。这样缺乏管教的行为，但望能够有所矫正——纵然不图更高的体面，也算顾全这个顽劣孩子所属的那一家的规矩名声。

===Original===
Georgie had muttered various interruptions, and as she concluded the reading he said: "He's an ole liar!"

"Georgie, you mustn't say 'liar.' Isn't this letter the truth?"

"Well," said Georgie, "how old am I?"

"Ten."

"Well, look how he says I'm older than a boy eleven years old."

===Chinese===
小乔治嘟嘟囔囔，打了不少岔；等她念完，他说：“他是个老骗子！”

“小乔治，不许说‘骗子’。这封信说的难道不是实情？”

“那好，”小乔治说，“我几岁？”

“十岁。”

“那你瞧瞧，他说我比一个十一岁的孩子还大。”

===Original===
"That's true," said Isabel. "He does. But isn't some of it true, Georgie?"

Georgie felt himself to be in a difficulty here, and he was silent.

"Georgie, did you say what he says you did?"

"Which one?"

"Did you tell him to—to—Did you say, 'Go to hell'?"

===Chinese===
“这倒是真的，”伊莎贝尔说。“他是这么写的。可总有些地方是真的吧，小乔治？”

小乔治觉得自己这会儿有些下不来台，便不吭声了。

“小乔治，他信里说你说的那些话，你到底说了没有？”

“哪一句？”

“你是不是叫他——叫他——你是不是说了‘下地狱去吧’？”

===Original===
Georgie looked worried for a moment longer; then he brightened. "Listen here, mamma; grandpa wouldn't wipe his shoe on that ole storyteller, would he?"

"Georgie, you mustn't—"

"I mean: none of the Ambersons wouldn't have anything to do with him, would they? He doesn't even know *you*, does he, mamma?"

"That hasn't anything to do with it."

===Chinese===
小乔治又愁了一小会儿，忽然眼睛一亮。“妈，你听我说；外公连鞋底都不会往那个老编瞎话的身上蹭一下吧，会不会？”

“小乔治，你不许——”

“我是说：安伯森家没有哪一个肯跟他沾一点儿边，对吧？他连‘你’都不认识呢，对不对，妈？”

“这跟那件事不相干。”

===Original===
"Yes, it has! I mean: none of the Amberson family go to see him, and they never have him come in their house; they wouldn't ask him to, and they prob'ly wouldn't even let him."

"That isn't what we're talking about."

"I bet," said Georgie emphatically, "I bet if he wanted to see any of 'em, he'd haf to go around to the side door!"

"No, dear, they—"

"Yes, they would, mamma! So what does it matter if I did say somep'm' to him he didn't like? That kind o' people, I don't see why you can't say anything you want to, to 'em!"

===Chinese===
“怎么不相干！我是说：安伯森家没有谁去看他，也从来不肯让他进自家的门；他们不会请他来，多半连他上门都不让进。”

“我们说的不是这个。”

“我敢打赌，”小乔治斩钉截铁地说，“我敢打赌，他要是想见安伯森家随便哪一位，就只配绕到边门去！”

“不是的，亲爱的，他们——”

“就是的，妈！那我说了句他不中听的话，又有什么打紧？对那种人，我就不明白，为什么想说什么还不能说！”

===Original===
"No, Georgie. And you haven't answered me whether you said that dreadful thing he says you did."

"Well—" said Georgie. "Anyway, he said somep'm' to me that made me mad." And upon this point he offered no further details; he would not explain to his mother that what had made him "mad" was Mr. Smith's hasty condemnation of herself: "Your mother ought to be ashamed," and, "A woman that lets a bad boy like you—" Georgie did not even consider excusing himself by quoting these insolences.

Isabel stroked his head. "They were terrible words for you to use, dear. From his letter he doesn't seem a very tactful person, but—"

"He's just riffraff," said Georgie.

===Chinese===
“不行，小乔治。他信里说你说的那句难听话，你到底说了没有，你还没有回答我呢。”

“这个嘛——”小乔治说。“反正，他说了一句什么话，把我惹翻了。”至于这一层，他不肯再多透半个字；他决计不向母亲解释：他之所以给“惹翻”，是因为史密斯先生一张嘴就糟蹋了她本人——“你母亲自己就该害臊”，还有“一个女人，放任你这么个坏小子——”。拿这几句无礼的话来替自己开脱，小乔治连想都没有想过。

伊莎贝尔抚着他的头发。“那些脏字眼，出自你的嘴可不叫话，亲爱的。看他信上的意思，他这人不大识体统，不过——”

“他就是个下三烂。”小乔治说。

===Original===
"You mustn't say so," his mother gently agreed. "Where did you learn those bad words he speaks of? Where did you hear anyone use them?"

"Well, I've heard 'em several places. I guess Uncle George Amberson was the *first* I ever heard say 'em. Uncle George Amberson said 'em to papa once. Papa didn't like it, but Uncle George was just laughin' at papa, an' then he said 'em while he was laughin'."

"That was wrong of him," she said, but almost instinctively he detected the lack of conviction in her tone. It was Isabel's great failing that whatever an Amberson did seemed right to her, especially if the Amberson was either her brother George, or her son George. She knew that she should be more severe with the latter now, but severity with him was beyond her power; and the Reverend Malloch Smith had succeeded only in rousing her resentment against himself. Georgie's symmetrical face—altogether an Amberson face—had looked never more beautiful to her. It always looked unusually beautiful when she tried to be severe with him. "You must promise me," she said feebly, "never to use those bad words again."

"I promise not to," he said promptly—and he whispered an immediate codicil under his breath: "Unless I get mad at somebody!" This satisfied a code according to which, in his own sincere belief, he never told lies.

===Chinese===
“不许这么说，”他母亲温温柔柔地表示了赞同。“他信里说的那些脏话，你是打哪儿学来的？你听什么人说过？”

“呃，好些地方我都听人说过。我‘头一个’听见的，大概要算乔治·安伯森舅舅。舅舅有一回就冲爸爸说过。爸爸不爱听，可舅舅原本就是拿爸爸打趣，笑着笑着就说出来了。”

“那是他的不是，”她说，可他几乎出于本能，就听出了她话音里那点虚。伊莎贝尔的大毛病就在这里：安伯森家的人，做什么在她眼里都对，尤其是这位安伯森若是她的兄弟乔治，或是她的儿子乔治。她知道自己这会儿应该更加严厉才是，可对他严厉，她实在无此能力；马洛奇·史密斯牧师唯一达成的，是把她的一腔恼火反拨到了他自己身上。小乔治那张五官匀称的脸——地地道道一张安伯森家的脸——在她眼里从来没有这么好看过。而每逢她想要对他严厉，这张脸总显得分外好看。“你要答应我，”她有气无力地说，“再不说那些脏话了。”

“我答应不说。”他答得飞快——随即又压着嗓子，飞快地补上一条附款：“除非我叫谁给惹翻了！”照他自奉的这一套规矩，这样两下就两全了：他真心实意地相信，自己从来没有撒过谎。

===Original===
"That's a good boy," she said, and he ran out to the yard, his punishment over. Some admiring friends were gathered there; they had heard of his adventure, knew of the note, and were waiting to see what was going to "happen" to him. They hoped for an account of things, and also that he would allow them to "take turns" riding his pony to the end of the alley and back.

They were really his henchmen: Georgie was a lord among boys. In fact, he was a personage among certain sorts of grown people, and was often fawned upon; the alley negroes delighted in him, chuckled over him, flattered him slavishly. For that matter, he often heard well-dressed people speaking of him admiringly: a group of ladies once gathered about him on the pavement where he was spinning a top. "I *know* this is Georgie!" one exclaimed, and turned to the others with the impressiveness of a showman. "Major Amberson's only grandchild!" The others said, "It *is*?" and made clicking sounds with their mouths; two of them loudly whispering, "*So* handsome!"

Georgie, annoyed because they kept standing upon the circle he had chalked for his top, looked at them coldly and offered a suggestion:

"Oh, go hire a hall!"

===Chinese===
“这才是个乖孩子，”她说。他一溜烟跑进了院子，刑罚就此完结。院子里已经聚了几位仰慕他的小友；他们都听说了他的壮举，也知道有字条这回事，正等着瞧他会“遭”上什么事情。他们盼着听他讲讲来龙去脉，还盼着他恩准大家“轮流”骑他的小马，跑到巷子口再跑回来。

他们其实是他的人马：小乔治在孩子堆里是位王爷。说实话，在某些大人中间，他也算个人物，常有人对他曲意逢迎；小巷里的黑人们喜欢他，围着他乐，奴颜婢膝地奉承他。也难怪，连衣着体面的人里头，他也常听见有人带着激赏的口气讲起他：有一回，一群太太围上人行道来，当时他正在那儿抽陀螺。“我‘保准’这就是小乔治！”一位嚷道，转身朝着众人，拿出报幕人的架势。“安伯森少校唯一的外孙呀！”其余的人应道：“‘是嘛？’”嘴里啧啧连声；其中两位高声地交头接耳：“‘真俊呐！’”

小乔治正恼火她们一个劲儿踩住他用粉笔画好的陀螺圈，便冷冷地看了她们一眼，出了个主意：

“哼，租座礼堂演讲去吧！”

===Original===
As an Amberson, he was already a public character, and the story of his adventure in the Reverend Malloch Smith's front yard became a town topic. Many people glanced at him with great distaste, thereafter, when they chanced to encounter him, which meant nothing to Georgie, because he innocently believed most grown people to be necessarily cross-looking as a normal phenomenon resulting from the adult state; and he failed to comprehend that the distasteful glances had any personal bearing upon himself. If he had perceived such a bearing, he would have been affected only so far, probably, as to mutter, "Riffraff!" Possibly he would have shouted it; and, certainly, most people believed a story that went round the town just after Mrs. Amberson's funeral, when Georgie was eleven. Georgie was reported to have differed with the undertaker about the seating of the family; his indignant voice had become audible: "Well, who is the most important person at my own grandmother's funeral?" And later he had projected his head from the window of the foremost mourners' carriage, as the undertaker happened to pass.

"Riffraff!"

There were people—grown people they were—who expressed themselves longingly: they *did* hope to live to see the day, they said, when that boy would get his comeuppance! (They used that honest word, so much better than "deserts," and not until many years later to be more clumsily rendered as "what is coming to him.") *Something* was bound to take him down, some day, and they only wanted to *be* there! But Georgie heard nothing of this, and the yearners for his taking down went unsatisfied, while their yearning grew the greater as the happy day of fulfilment was longer and longer postponed. His grandeur was not diminished by the Malloch Smith story; the rather it was increased, and among other children (especially among little girls) there was added to the prestige of his gilded position that diabolical glamour which must inevitably attend a boy who has told a minister to go to hell.

===Chinese===
身为安伯森家的人，他早已是个公众角色；他在马洛奇·史密斯牧师家前院的那场壮举，成了全城的话题。打那以后，许多人偶然撞见他，都要带着十二分的不快瞟他一眼；这在小乔治毫无意味，因为他天真地认定，大人多半横眉竖眼，那是成年以后自然而然的常态；至于那些不痛快的眼色同他本人有什么牵扯，他就领会不到了。即便领会到了，他顶多多半咕哝一声：“下三烂！”没准儿还会嚷出声来；而且，安伯森太太的丧礼刚过、小乔治十一岁那年，满城流传的那桩故事，大多数人都是信的。据说，小乔治为了家属席次怎么安排，同殡葬承办人起了争执，只听得他气愤愤的声音：“哼，在我亲外婆的丧礼上，顶要紧的人是谁？”后来，殡葬承办人恰巧打马车旁边经过，他又从送葬车队头一辆车的车窗里，把脑袋探了出来。

“下三烂！”

有一些人——都是些大人——说起话来一脸的盼头：他们说，就巴望自己能活到那一天，亲眼看着那孩子遭一回现世报！（他们用的是“现世报”那个老实的字眼，比“应得的下场”好得多；要到许多年之后，这个字眼才又被更笨拙地说成“他早晚有那一天”。）总归有什么东西，迟早会把他压下去；他们所求的，不过是“在场”亲眼一观！这些话小乔治一概没有听见；盼他栽跟头的人一直未能如愿，而如愿的吉期一日推得远过一日，他们的盼头也就一日旺过一日。马洛奇·史密斯那桩事并没有折他的威风，反倒替他添了光：在别的孩子中间（尤其是在小姑娘们中间），他那镀金的地位之上，又平添了一层魔性的光彩——一个敢叫牧师“下地狱”的男孩，这层光彩是注定要追随着他的。

## III / 三

===Original===
Until he reached the age of twelve, Georgie’s education was a domestic process; tutors came to the house; and those citizens who yearned for his taking down often said: “Just wait till he has to go to public school; *then* he’ll get it!” But at twelve Georgie was sent to a private school in the town, and there came from this small and dependent institution no report, or even rumour, of Georgie’s getting anything that he was thought to deserve; therefore the yearning still persisted, though growing gaunt with feeding upon itself. For, although Georgie’s pomposities and impudence in the little school were often almost unbearable, the teachers were fascinated by him. They did not like him—he was too arrogant for that—but he kept them in such a state of emotion that they thought more about him than they did about all of the other ten pupils. The emotion he kept them in was usually one resulting from injured self-respect, but sometimes it was dazzled admiration. So far as their conscientious observation went, he “studied” his lessons sparingly; but sometimes, in class, he flashed an admirable answer, with a comprehension not often shown by the pupils they taught; and he passed his examinations easily. In all, without discernible effort, he acquired at this school some rudiments of a liberal education and learned nothing whatever about himself.

The yearners were still yearning when Georgie, at sixteen, was sent away to a great “Prep School.” “Now,” they said brightly, “he’ll get it! He’ll find himself among boys just as important in their home towns as he is, and they’ll knock the stuffing out of him when he puts on his airs with them! Oh, but that *would* be worth something to see!” They were mistaken, it appeared, for when Georgie returned, a few months later, he still seemed to have the same stuffing. He had been deported by the authorities, the offense being stated as “insolence and profanity”; in fact, he had given the principal of the school instructions almost identical with those formerly objected to by the Reverend Malloch Smith.

But he had not got his comeuppance, and those who counted upon it were embittered by his appearance upon the downtown streets driving a dogcart at criminal speed, making pedestrians retreat from the crossings, and behaving generally as if he “owned the earth.” A disgusted hardware dealer of middle age, one of those who hungered for Georgie’s downfall, was thus driven back upon the sidewalk to avoid being run over, and so far forgot himself as to make use of the pet street insult of the year: “Got ’ny *sense*! See here, bub, does your mother know you’re out?”

===Chinese===
到十二岁为止，乔吉（Georgie）的教育一直在家里进行；家庭教师登门授课。城里那些一心巴望看他碰壁的市民常说：“就等着瞧吧，等他非去上公立学校不可的那天，*那*他就有苦头吃了！”可到了十二岁，乔吉被送进了城里的一所私立学校，而这所规模既小、又仰人鼻息的学校，却不曾传出任何消息，连谣言都没有，说他吃了众人认定他该吃的苦头；于是这份巴望依然延续着，只是靠吞食自身过活，日渐瘦瘪。因为，尽管乔吉在那所小学校里的装腔作势和放肆无礼常常叫人几乎忍无可忍，老师们却个个被他迷住了。他们并不喜欢他——他傲慢得让人喜欢不起来——但他叫他们始终处在一种情绪激荡的状态里，惦记他的心思比惦记其余十名学生加起来还多。他让他们处的这种情绪，通常源于自尊心受了伤，有时却是令人目眩的倾慕。在这些兢兢业业的观察者看来，他的功课“念”得相当节制；但有时在课堂上，他会倏地闪出一句令人击节的答案，透出一种在他们所教的学生中不常见到的领悟力；而且考试他都轻松通过。总之，他不露一丝辛苦，便在这所学校里学到了一些自由教育的入门功课，至于认识他自己，却一星半点也没学到。

巴望的人们仍在巴望。乔吉十六岁那年，被送去了一所了不起的“预科学校”（Prep School）。“这下好了，”他们眉飞色舞地说，“他可有苦头吃啦！他会发觉自己落到一群在家乡跟他同样了不起的男孩中间，等他冲人家摆起架子，人家就要敲掉他那份神气！哦呀，那*才*值得一看呢！”看样子他们想错了：几个月后乔吉回来时，那份神气似乎原封未动。他是让校方“递解”回来的，罪名列作“倨傲无礼、口出秽言”；实际上，他给校长下的那番指示，与当年马洛克·史密斯牧师（Reverend Malloch Smith）所不能容忍的那番几乎如出一辙。

可他到底没挨着他那份报应，指望他挨报应的人倒叫他气苦了：他驾着一辆轻便双轮马车在闹市街口出现，车速快得近于犯罪，逼得行人纷纷从路口退避，一举一动俨然地球是他家的产业。一位满心厌恶的中年五金商人——他正是巴望乔吉栽跟头的人之一——为了免遭碾压，被逼退到人行道上，一时忘乎所以，骂出了本年度街头最时兴的那句损话：“有没有*脑子*！喂，小家伙，你妈知道你跑出来了吗？”

===Original===
Georgie, without even seeming to look at him, flicked the long lash of his whip dexterously, and a little spurt of dust came from the hardware man’s trousers, not far below the waist. He was not made of hardware: he raved, looking for a missile; then, finding none, commanded himself sufficiently to shout after the rapid dogcart: “Turn down your pants, you would-be dude! Raining in dear ole Lunnon! Git off the earth!”

Georgie gave him no encouragement to think that he was heard. The dogcart turned the next corner, causing indignation there, likewise, and, having proceeded some distance farther, halted in front of the “Amberson Block”—an old-fashioned four-story brick warren of lawyers’ offices, insurance and real estate offices, with a “drygoods store” occupying the ground floor. Georgie tied his lathered trotter to a telegraph pole, and stood for a moment looking at the building critically: it seemed shabby, and he thought his grandfather ought to replace it with a fourteen-story skyscraper, or even a higher one, such as he had lately seen in New York—when he stopped there for a few days of recreation and rest on his way home from the bereaved school. About the entryway to the stairs were various tin signs, announcing the occupation and location of upper-floor tenants, and Georgie decided to take some of these with him if he should ever go to college. However, he did not stop to collect them at this time, but climbed the worn stairs—there was no elevator—to the fourth floor, went down a dark corridor, and rapped three times upon a door. It was a mysterious door, its upper half, of opaque glass, bearing no sign to state the business or profession of the occupants within; but overhead, upon the lintel, four letters had been smearingly inscribed, partly with purple ink and partly with a soft lead pencil, “F.O.T.A.” and upon the plaster wall, above the lintel, there was a drawing dear to male adolescence: a skull and crossbones.

Three raps, similar to Georgie’s, sounded from within the room. Georgie then rapped four times; the rapper within the room rapped twice, and Georgie rapped seven times. This ended precautionary measures; and a well-dressed boy of sixteen opened the door; whereupon Georgie entered quickly, and the door was closed behind him. Seven boys of congenial age were seated in a semicircular row of damaged office chairs, facing a platform whereon stood a solemn, red-haired young personage with a table before him. At one end of the room there was a battered sideboard, and upon it were some empty beer bottles, a tobacco can about two-thirds full, with a web of mold over the surface of the tobacco, a dusty cabinet photograph (not inscribed) of Miss Lillian Russell, several withered old pickles, a caseknife, and a half-petrified section of icing-cake on a sooty plate. At the other end of the room were two rickety card-tables and a stand of bookshelves where were displayed under dust four or five small volumes of M. Guy de Maupassant’s stories, *Robinson Crusoe*, Sappho, *Mr. Barnes of New York*, a work by Giovanni Boccaccio, a Bible, *The Arabian Nights’ Entertainment*, *Studies of the Human Form Divine*, *The Little Minister*, and a clutter of monthly magazines and illustrated weeklies of about that crispness one finds in such articles upon a doctor’s anteroom table. Upon the wall, above the sideboard, was an old framed lithograph of Miss Della Fox in *Wang*; over the bookshelves there was another lithograph purporting to represent Mr. John L. Sullivan in a boxing costume, and beside it a halftone reproduction of *A Reading from Homer*. The final decoration consisted of damaged papiermache—a round shield with two battle-axes and two cross-hilted swords, upon the wall over the little platform where stood the red-haired presiding officer. He addressed Georgie in a serious voice:

===Chinese===
乔吉连看都像没有看他，只灵巧地一抖长长的鞭梢，五金商人的裤子上，腰部下方不远的地方，便腾起一小股尘土。此人可不是五金铸成的：他暴跳如雷，四下寻找可以投掷的东西；一无所获，这才强自镇定下来，朝着疾驰而去的马车背影喊道：“把裤腿放下来吧，你这自命不凡的纨绔哥儿！亲爱的老伦敦下雨啦！滚出地球去吧！”

乔吉没有给他半点自己听见了的表示。双轮马车拐过下一个街角，同样在当地激起一腔公愤，又向前跑了一程，停在“安伯森大楼”（Amberson Block）门前——一幢老式的四层砖造蜂窝，里面挤满律师事务所、保险行和地产行，底层由一家“布匹杂货店”占着。乔吉把那匹汗沫淋漓的马拴在一根电线杆上，站着对这座楼挑剔地端详了片刻：它显得寒碜；他想他外祖父理应把它拆掉，换成一幢十四层的摩天大楼，或者更高，就像他最近在纽约见到的那种——那是他从那所痛失爱生的学校回家的路上，顺道在纽约停留几天、消遣休憩时看见的。楼梯入口四周挂着各式铁皮招牌，标明楼上房客的行当和所在；乔吉暗自决定，哪天他要是上大学去，动身时就捎上几块留作纪念。不过这一回他没有驻足收取，而是登上磨损的楼梯——没有电梯——到了四楼，沿一条昏暗的走廊走到头，在一扇门上敲了三下。那是一扇神秘的门：上半截是磨砂玻璃，没有任何标明屋内人营生或职业的牌子；只是门楣上方，用紫墨水混着软铅笔，歪歪扭扭涂抹着四个字母：“F.O.T.A.”；门楣上方的灰泥墙上，还画着一幅男少年们最心爱的图画：一副骷髅底下交叉着两根骨头。

屋里传出三下敲门声，跟乔吉敲的一样。乔吉便敲四下；屋里那位回敲两下，乔吉再敲七下。预防措施至此为止；一个衣着讲究的十六岁男孩开了门，乔吉迅速闪身进去，门在身后关上。七个年纪相仿的男孩坐成一排半圆形的破旧办公椅，面对一座小讲台，台上站着一位神色庄重的红发少年要人，面前摆着一张桌子。房间一头立着一只遍体鳞伤的餐具柜，柜上摆着几个空啤酒瓶、一只约莫还剩三分之二烟丝的铁皮烟罐——烟丝表面结着一层霉网——一张蒙尘的柜式大照片（无题字），相中人是莉莲·拉塞尔（Lillian Russell）小姐，另有几条干瘪的老酸黄瓜、一把餐刀，还有烟熏火燎的盘子里半石化的一角糖霜蛋糕。房间另一头是两张摇摇晃晃的牌桌和一个书架，架上蒙尘陈列着四五册居伊·德·莫泊桑（Guy de Maupassant）先生的故事集、《鲁滨逊漂流记》、《萨福》、《纽约的巴恩斯先生》、乔万尼·薄伽丘（Giovanni Boccaccio）的一部著作、一部《圣经》、《一千零一夜》、《神圣人体习作》、《小牧师》，外加一堆月刊和画刊周报，脆得跟大夫候诊室桌上那些旧刊物一个成色。餐具柜上方墙上，挂着一幅装了框的旧石版画，画的是黛拉·福克斯（Della Fox）小姐在《王》（Wang）里的模样；书架上方另一幅石版画，据说画的是身着拳击服的约翰·L·沙利文（John L. Sullivan）先生，旁边是一幅《朗诵荷马》（A Reading from Homer）的网目版复制品。最后的装饰是几件破损的纸浆道具——一面圆盾、两柄战斧、两柄十字柄剑——钉在红发主持官所站小讲台上方的墙上。他用郑重其事的声音向乔吉致辞：

===Original===
“Welcome, Friend of the Ace.”

“Welcome, Friend of the Ace,” Georgie responded, and all of the other boys repeated the words, “Welcome, Friend of the Ace.”

“Take your seat in the secret semicircle,” said the presiding officer. “We will now proceed to—”

But Georgie was disposed to be informal. He interrupted, turning to the boy who had admitted him: “Look here, Charlie Johnson, what’s Fred Kinney doing in the president’s chair? That’s my place, isn’t it? What you men been up to here, anyhow? Didn’t you all agree I was to be president just the same, even if I was away at school?”

“Well—” said Charlie Johnson uneasily. “Listen! I didn’t have much to do with it. Some of the other members thought that long as you weren’t in town or anything, and Fred gave the sideboard, why—”

===Chinese===
“欢迎，爱司之友（Friend of the Ace）。”

“欢迎，爱司之友。”乔吉应道，其余男孩一齐复诵：“欢迎，爱司之友。”

“请在秘密半圆席上就座，”主持官说，“我们现在开始进行——”

可乔吉有意不拘礼数。他打断话头，转向替他开门的男孩：“我说，查理·约翰逊（Charlie Johnson），弗雷德·金尼（Fred Kinney）怎么坐上主席的椅子了？那位置不是我的吗？你们这帮人在这儿到底搞了什么名堂？不是大伙儿都答应好了吗——就算我去外地上学，主席照样归我当？”

“呃——”查理·约翰逊不安地说，“你听我说！这事我可没出多少力。别的几个会员认为，反正你不在城里待着嘛，餐具柜又是弗雷德捐的，所以——”

===Original===
Mr. Kinney, presiding, held in his hand, in lieu of a gavel, and considered much more impressive, a Civil War relic known as a “horse-pistol.” He rapped loudly for order. “All Friends of the Ace will take their seats!” he said sharply. “I’m president of the F.O.T.A. now, George Minafer, and don’t you forget it! You and Charlie Johnson sit down, because I was elected perfectly fair, and we’re goin’ to hold a meeting here.”

“Oh, you are, are you?” said George skeptically.

Charlie Johnson thought to mollify him. “Well, didn’t we call this meeting just especially because you told us to? You said yourself we ought to have a kind of celebration because you’ve got back to town, George, and that’s what we’re here for now, and everything. What do you care about being president? All it amounts to is just calling the roll and—”

The president de facto hammered the table. “This meeting will now proceed to—”

“No, it won’t,” said George, and he advanced to the desk, laughing contemptuously. “Get off that platform.”

===Chinese===
主持会议的金尼先生手里握着一件内战遗物，人称“骑兵手枪”（horse-pistol），权充议长槌——而且公认要气派得多。他大声敲着桌子要大家肃静。“全体爱司之友各自就座！”他厉声道，“如今爱司之友会（F.O.T.A.）的主席是我，乔治·米纳弗（George Minafer），你给我记住了！你和查理·约翰逊坐下，我这主席选得完全光明正大，我们今天就在这儿开会。”

“哦，你是主席，是吗？”乔治满腹狐疑地说。

查理·约翰逊想着息事宁人。“呃，这个会不正是单为你发了话才专门召集的吗？你自己说的，你回城了，大家该有个小小的庆祝，乔治，我们如今聚在这儿就是为了这个，如此而已。当主席这事儿你在乎什么？说到底不过就是点点名，再——”

这位事实上的主席把桌子捶得砰砰响。“本会议现在开始进行——”

“不，开始不了，”乔治说着，带着轻蔑的笑容走向桌前。“从讲台上下来。”

===Original===
“This meeting will come to order!” Mr. Kinney commanded fiercely.

“You put down that gavel,” said George. “Whose is it, I’d like to know? It belongs to my grandfather, and you quit hammering it that way or you’ll break it, and I’ll have to knock your head off.”

“This meeting will come to order! I was legally elected here, and I’m not going to be bulldozed!”

“All right,” said Georgie. “You’re president. Now we’ll hold another election.”

“We will not!” Fred Kinney shouted. “We’ll have our reg’lar meeting, and then we’ll play euchre a nickel a corner, what we’re here for. This meeting will now come to ord—”

===Chinese===
“本会议现在肃静！”金尼先生声色俱厉地喝令。

“你把那把槌放下，”乔治说，“我倒想问问，它是谁的？那是我外祖父的东西，你再这么敲下去，敲坏了它，我可就得敲掉你的脑袋了。”

“本会议现在肃静！我是经合法程序选出来的，谁也别想吓唬我！”

“好啊，”乔吉说，“你是主席。那我们现在再举行一回选举。”

“不举行！”弗雷德·金尼吼道，“咱们要开完例会，然后打尤克牌，每角押五分钱——咱们来就为的这个。本会议现在肃——”

===Original===
Georgie addressed the members. “I’d like to know who got up this thing in the first place,” he said. “Who’s the founder of the F.O.T.A., if you please? Who got this room rent free? Who got the janitor to let us have most of this furniture? You suppose you could keep this clubroom a minute if I told my grandfather I didn’t want it for a literary club any more? I’d like to say a word on how you members been acting, too! When I went away I said I didn’t care if you had a *vice*-president or something while I was gone, but here I hardly turned my back and you had to go and elect Fred Kinney president! Well, if that’s what you want, you can have it. I was going to have a little celebration down here some night pretty soon, and bring some port wine, like we drink at school in our crowd there, and I *was* going to get my grandfather to give the club an extra room across the hall, and prob’ly I could get my Uncle George to give us his old billiard table, because he’s got a new one, and the club could put it in the other room. Well, you got a new president now!” Here Georgie moved toward the door and his tone became plaintive, though undeniably there was disdain beneath his sorrow. “I guess all I better do is—resign!”

And he opened the door, apparently intending to withdraw.

“All in favour of having a new election,” Charlie Johnson shouted hastily, “say, ‘Aye’!”

“Aye” was said by everyone present except Mr. Kinney, who began a hot protest, but it was immediately smothered.

===Chinese===
乔吉面向全体会员讲话。“我很想知道，当初把这张罗起来的是谁，”他说，“劳驾，爱司之友会的创始人是谁？是谁弄到这间屋子不要房租的？是谁说动门房把这里大部分家具借给我们的？要是我跟我外祖父说，我不再拿这儿当文学社了，你们以为这间俱乐部活动室你们还能占上一分钟？会员们这阵子的做派，我也想说一句！我走的时候说过，我不在乎你们在我离开期间设个*副*主席什么的，可我前脚刚转身，你们后脚就非得选弗雷德·金尼当主席！好哇，既然你们要这个，那就拿去吧。我本来打算最近哪天晚上在这儿办个小庆祝会，带点波尔图葡萄酒来，像我们在学校里那伙人喝的那样；我*本来*还打算让我外祖父把大厅对面的另一个房间也给俱乐部，很可能还能让我乔治舅舅把他那张旧台球桌送给我们——反正他已经有张新的了——俱乐部可以把它摆到那间屋里去。好啊，如今你们有新主席啦！”说到这里，乔吉朝门口走去，语调变得哀怨，尽管悲愤底下分明压着一层轻蔑。“我想我顶好是——辞职！”

说着他拉开房门，看那意思显然是要退会而去。

“赞成重新举行选举的，”查理·约翰逊急忙高喊，“请说‘赞成’！”

在场的人一齐说“赞成”，唯独金尼先生除外；他刚要发一通火辣辣的抗议，那抗议立刻便被闷了回去。

===Original===
“All in favour of me being president instead of Fred Kinney,” shouted Georgie, “say ‘Aye.’ The ‘Ayes’ have it!”

“I resign,” said the redheaded boy, gulping as he descended from the platform. “I resign from the club!”

Hot-eyed, he found his hat and departed, jeers echoing after him as he plunged down the corridor. Georgie stepped upon the platform, and took up the emblem of office.

“Ole redhead Fred’ll be around next week,” said the new chairman. “He’ll be around boot-lickin’ to get us to take him back in again, but I guess we don’t want him: that fellow always was a troublemaker. We will now proceed with our meeting. Well, fellows, I suppose you want to hear from your president. I don’t know that I have much to say, as I have already seen most of you a few times since I got back. I had a good time at the old school, back East, but had a little trouble with the faculty and came on home. My family stood by me as well as I could ask, and I expect to stay right here in the old town until whenever I decide to enter college. Now, I don’t suppose there’s any more business before the meeting. I guess we might as well play cards. Anybody that’s game for a little quarter-limit poker or any limit they say, why I’d like to have ’em sit at the president’s card-table.”

===Chinese===
“赞成由我顶替弗雷德·金尼出任主席的，”乔吉高喊，“说‘赞成’。‘赞成’票占多数，通过！”

“我辞职，”红发男孩说着，喉头哽咽地走下讲台。“我退出本会！”

他两眼冒火，找到帽子便走，一头扎下走廊，讥笑声在他身后回响不绝。乔吉登上讲台，拿起了那件职位的标志物。

“红毛弗雷德下个礼拜就会转悠回来，”新任主席说，“他会舔着靴子求咱们重新收留他，不过我看咱们不要他：那家伙从来就是个搅局精。现在我们继续开会。好了，伙计们，想必大家都想听听主席讲话。我说不出有什么可多讲的，因为回来之后，你们大多数人我已经见过几面了。在东部那所老学校我过得挺痛快，就是跟教职员闹了点小别扭，便回家了。家里人待我，不拘怎么要求都不曾让我失望，我打算就待在这座老城里，直到哪天我决定进大学为止。好了，我看会里再没有别的议程了。咱们不如打牌吧。哪位有胆量来一局两毛五限注的小扑克——或者注码随他们开——就请到主席的牌桌上来坐。”

===Original===
When the diversions of the Friends of the Ace were concluded for that afternoon, Georgie invited his chief supporter, Mr. Charlie Johnson, to drive home with him to dinner, and as they jingled up National Avenue in the dogcart, Charlie asked:

“What sort of men did you run up against at that school, George?”

“Best crowd there: finest set of men I ever met.”

“How’d you get in with ’em?”

===Chinese===
那天下午，爱司之友们的余兴散场之后，乔吉邀了他那位头号拥戴者查理·约翰逊先生同乘马车回家吃晚饭；当双轮马车叮叮当当地驶上国民大道（National Avenue）时，查理问道：

“你在那所学校碰上的都是些什么样的人，乔治？”

“那儿最拔尖的一伙：我这辈子见过的最出色的一群人。”

“你怎么跟他们搭上的？”

===Original===
Georgie laughed. “I let them get in with *me*, Charlie,” he said in a tone of gentle explanation. “It’s vulgar to do any other way. Did I tell you the nickname they gave me—‘King’? That was what they called me at that school, ‘King Minafer.’ ”

“How’d they happen to do that?” his friend asked innocently.

“Oh, different things,” George answered lightly. “Of course, any of ’em that came from anywhere out in this part the country knew about the family and all that, and so I suppose it was a good deal on account of—oh, on account of the family and the way I do things, most likely.”

===Chinese===
乔吉笑了。“我是让他们来跟*我*结交的，查理，”他用一种温言软语、解释般的口气说，“换别的做法就俗气了。我跟你说没说他们给我起的绰号——‘王’？在那所学校里他们都这么叫我，‘米纳弗王’。”

“他们怎么会起这么个叫法呢？”他的朋友天真地问。

“哦，种种缘故吧，”乔治轻描淡写地答道，“当然，他们当中凡是从中西部这一带任何地方来的，都知道我们这家子和那一套，所以我想多半是因为——哦，多半是因为家世，再加上我办事的路数吧。”

## IV / 四

===Original===
When Mr. George Amberson Minafer came home for the holidays at Christmastide, in his sophomore year, probably no great change had taken place inside him, but his exterior was visibly altered. Nothing about him encouraged any hope that he had received his comeuppance; on the contrary, the yearners for that stroke of justice must yearn even more itchingly: the gilded youth's manner had become polite, but his politeness was of a kind which democratic people found hard to bear. In a word, M. le Due had returned from the gay life of the capital to show himself for a week among the loyal peasants belonging to the old château, and their quaint habits and costumes afforded him a mild amusement.

Cards were out for a ball in his honour, and this pageant of the tenantry was held in the ballroom of the Amberson Mansion the night after his arrival. It was, as Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster said of Isabel's wedding, "a big Amberson-style thing," though that wise Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster had long ago gone the way of all wisdom, having stepped out of the Midland town, unquestionably into heaven—a long step, but not beyond her powers. She had successors, but no successor; the town having grown too large to confess that it was intellectually led and literarily authoritated by one person; and some of these successors were not invited to the ball, for dimensions were now so metropolitan that intellectual leaders and literary authorities loomed in outlying regions unfamiliar to the Ambersons. However, all "old citizens" recognizable as gentry received cards, and of course so did their dancing descendants.

The orchestra and the caterer were brought from away, in the Amberson manner, though this was really a gesture—perhaps one more of habit than of ostentation—for servitors of gaiety as proficient as these importations were nowadays to be found in the town. Even flowers and plants and roped vines were brought from afar—not, however, until the stock of the local florists proved insufficient to obliterate the interior structure of the big house, in the Amberson way. It was the last of the great, long remembered dances that "everybody talked about"—there were getting to be so many people in town that no later than the next year there were too many for "everybody" to hear of even such a ball as the Ambersons'.

===Chinese===
乔治·安伯森·米纳弗（George Amberson Minafer）先生大学二年级那年圣诞时节放假回家，内里大概并没有发生多大变化，外表却分明变了样。他浑身上下没有一处能让人指望他已吃到报应；恰恰相反，盼着这记公道落到他头上的诸位，只会越发盼得心里发痒：这位纨绔子弟的做派变得客气了，可他那种客气法，偏偏是讲平等的大众最难消受的一种。一句话，杜克老爷（M. le Duc）从京城的欢场回到旧日城堡，要在忠于城堡的忠顺庄稼人中间露一星期的脸；这班人古怪的习气和行头，也给他平添了一桩淡淡的乐子。

为向他致敬的舞会已经发出请柬；这场佃户们的盛典，在他抵达后的第二天晚上，在安伯森公馆（the Amberson Mansion）的舞厅里举行。用亨利·富兰克林·福斯特太太（Mrs. Henry Franklin Foster）当年形容伊莎贝尔（Isabel）婚礼的话说，这是“一场十足安伯森派头的盛事”；只可惜那位智慧的福斯特太太，早已步了天下智者终须一走的后尘——从中部小城迈步出去，毫无疑问迈进了天堂。这一步迈得不可谓不远，倒也难不倒她。接她班的人有，接得下她衣钵的人却没有；城已经大得不肯承认自己的思想由一个人引领、文坛由一个人做主了；何况这班后继者里，有些压根没接到舞会请柬——如今城地面大得有了都会气派，思想领袖和文学权威们隐隐现身在偏远地带，安伯森家的人连那些地方都没听说过。不过，凡看得出是士绅的“老住户”都收到了请柬，他们那些会跳舞的后辈自然也在其列。

乐队和酒席承办人是照安伯森家的老规矩从外地请来的，虽说这其实不过是个姿态——与其说是炫耀，不如说是积习——因为这样娴熟的行乐侍候人手，如今城里本也寻得着。连花卉、绿植和缠扎成架的藤蔓都是从远处运来的——不过得先等本地花匠的存货证明不够把大宅的内部结构遮个干净，才照安伯森家的路数去运。这是“人人都说起来”的、日后会长久被记起的大舞会中最后的一场——城里的人越聚越多，不到第二年，人就已多到“人人”都未必听得全安伯森家这样的舞会了。

===Original===
George, white-gloved, with a gardenia in his buttonhole, stood with his mother and the Major, embowered in the big red and gold drawing room downstairs, to "receive" the guests; and, standing thus together, the trio offered a picturesque example of good looks persistent through three generations. The Major, his daughter, and his grandson were of a type all Amberson: tall, straight, and regular, with dark eyes, short noses, good chins; and the grandfather's expression, no less than the grandson's, was one of faintly amused condescension. There was a difference, however. The grandson's unlined young face had nothing to offer except this condescension; the grandfather's had other things to say. It was a handsome, worldly old face, conscious of its importance, but persuasive rather than arrogant, and not without tokens of sufferings withstood. The Major's short white hair was parted in the middle, like his grandson's, and in all he stood as briskly equipped to the fashion as exquisite young George.

Isabel, standing between her father and her son, caused a vague amazement in the mind of the latter. Her age, just under forty, was for George a thought of something as remote as the moons of Jupiter: he could not possibly have conceived such an age ever coming to be his own: five years was the limit of his thinking in time. Five years ago he had been a child not yet fourteen; and those five years were an abyss. Five years hence he would be almost twenty-four; what the girls he knew called "one of the older men." He could imagine himself at twenty-four, but beyond that, his powers staggered and refused the task. He saw little essential difference between thirty-eight and eighty-eight, and his mother was to him not a woman but wholly a mother. He had no perception of her other than as an adjunct to himself, his mother; nor could he imagine her thinking or doing anything—falling in love, walking with a friend, or reading a book—as a woman, and not as his mother. The woman, Isabel, was a stranger to her son; as completely a stranger as if he had never in his life seen her or heard her voice. And it was tonight, while he stood with her, "receiving," that he caught a disquieting glimpse of this stranger whom he thus fleetingly encountered for the first time.

Youth cannot imagine romance apart from youth. That is why the roles of the heroes and heroines of plays are given by the managers to the most youthful actors they can find among the competent. Both middle-aged people and young people enjoy a play about young lovers; but only middle-aged people will tolerate a play about middle-aged lovers; young people will not come to see such a play, because, for them, middle-aged lovers are a joke—not a very funny one. Therefore, to bring both the middle-aged people and the young people into his house, the manager makes his romance as young as he can.
