George looked at him with an air of inquiry, but said nothing. He had grown to be almost as silent as his grandfather. However, the Major spoke without being questioned.

“It must be in the sun,” he said. “There wasn’t anything here but the sun in the first place, and the earth came out of the sun, and we came out of the earth. So, whatever we are, we must have been in the sun. We go back to the earth we came out of, so the earth will go back to the sun that it came out of. And time means nothing—nothing at all—so in a little while we’ll all be back in the sun together. I wish—”

He moved his hand uncertainly as if reaching for something, and George jumped up. “Did you want anything, grandfather?”

===Chinese===
可是安伯森等得太久了。女儿故去以来，少校已经用去了十一个月，把要紧的事情想个明白。能想到的他都想到了；人世间已没有什么能再留他。一天晚上，他的外孙陪他坐着——单凭他们所能揣摩出的他的喜好来看，少校顶喜欢的还是让小乔治陪着——老先生做了个古怪的动作：他一拍膝盖，像是忽然有了什么发现，又像是记起自己忘了件什么事。

乔治带着询问的神气望着他，却没作声。他已经变得几乎同他外祖父一样沉默。然而，不等问，少校自己开了口。

“一定是在太阳里，”他说。“起先这世上什么都没有，只有太阳；地球是从太阳里出来的，我们又是从地球里出来的。所以，不管我们是什么，我们必定曾经在太阳里。我们要回到我们出来的那个地球上去，地球也终要回到它出来的那个太阳里去。而时间算不了什么——什么也算不上——所以要不了多久，我们就都一同回到太阳里去了。但愿——”

他迟疑地抬起手，像要够一件什么东西，乔治一跃而起。“外公，您想要什么吗？”

===Original===
“What?”

“Would you like a glass of water?”

“No—no. No; I don’t want anything.” The reaching hand dropped back upon the arm of his chair, and he relapsed into silence; but a few minutes later he finished the sentence he had begun:

“I wish—somebody could tell me!”

===Chinese===
“什么？”

“要不要喝杯水？”

“不——不。不；我什么也不要。”那只伸着的手落回椅子的扶手上，他重新陷入沉默；可是几分钟之后，他把起头那句话续完了：

“但愿——有人能告诉我！”

===Original===
The next day he had a slight cold, but he seemed annoyed when his son suggested calling the doctor, and Amberson let him have his own way so far, in fact, that after he had got up and dressed, the following morning, he was all alone when he went away to find out what he hadn’t been able to think out—all those things he had wished “somebody” would tell him.

Old Sam, shuffling in with the breakfast tray, found the Major in his accustomed easy-chair by the fireplace—and yet even the old darkey could see instantly that the Major was not there.

===Chinese===
第二天他着了点凉；可儿子提出去请医生，他倒像是着恼了。安伯森也就由着他，甚至由到了这般地步：次晨他起床穿衣之后，独自一人动身走了，去寻个明白——那些他没能想透的事，那些他曾盼着“有人”能告诉他的一切。

老山姆趿拉着脚步，端着早饭托盘进来，只见少校照旧坐在壁炉边他惯坐的安乐椅里——然而，就连这位年迈的黑人仆役也一眼看得出：少校已经不在那儿了。

## XXXI / 三十一

===Original===
When the great Amberson Estate went into court for settlement, “there wasn’t any,” George Amberson said—that is, when the settlement was concluded there was no estate. “I guessed it,” Amberson went on. “As an expert on prosperity, my career is disreputable, but as a prophet of calamity I deserve a testimonial banquet.” He reproached himself bitterly for not having long ago discovered that his father had never given Isabel a deed to her house. “And those pigs, Sydney and Amelia!” he added, for this was another thing he was bitter about. “They won’t do anything. I’m sorry I gave them the opportunity of making a polished refusal. Amelia’s letter was about half in Italian; she couldn’t remember enough ways of saying no in English. One has to live quite a long while to realize there *are* people like that! The estate was badly crippled, even before they took out their ‘third,’ and the ‘third’ they took was the only good part of the rotten apple. Well, I didn’t ask them for restitution on my own account, and at least it will save *you* some trouble, young George. Never waste any time writing to them; you mustn’t count on them.”

“I don’t,” George said quietly. “I don’t count on anything.”

“Oh, we’ll not feel that things are quite desperate,” Amberson laughed, but not with great cheerfulness. “We’ll survive, Georgie—*you* will, especially. For my part I’m a little too old and too accustomed to fall back on somebody else for supplies to start a big fight with life: I’ll be content with just surviving, and I can do it on an eighteen-hundred-dollar-a-year consulship. An ex-congressman can always be pretty sure of getting some such job, and I hear from Washington the matter’s about settled. I’ll live pleasantly enough with a pitcher of ice under a palm tree, and black folks to wait on me—that part of it will be like home—and I’ll manage to send you fifty dollars every now and then, after I once get settled. So much for me! But you—of course you’ve had a poor training for making your own way, but you’re only a boy after all, and the stuff of the old stock is in you. It’ll come out and do something. I’ll never forgive myself about that deed: it would have given you something substantial to start with. Still, you have a little tiny bit, and you’ll have a little tiny salary, too; and of course your Aunt Fanny’s here, and she’s got something you can fall back on if you get *too* pinched, until I can begin to send you a dribble now and then.”

===Chinese===
那份庞大的安伯森产业呈请法院清算的时候，“清算出来已经什么都没有了，”乔治·安伯森（George Amberson）说——意思是，等清算办结，这份产业已不复存在。“我早料到了，”乔治·安伯森接着说。“论做发财的行家，我这番履历声名狼藉；可论做灾祸的预言家，我够格受一席公宴的致敬。”他狠狠地自责，怪自己没有早些发现，父亲从来不曾给伊莎贝尔立过她那所房子的契据。“还有那两头猪，悉尼和阿米莉亚！”他又添上一句，这也是他痛心的一桩。“他们什么都不肯出。我懊悔给了他们机会，让他们把回绝说得那样漂亮。阿米莉亚那封信，差不多一半用的是意大利文——用英语说‘不’的说法，她记不全那么多花样。人得活上大半辈子，才会明白世上*真有*这样的人！这份产业早就伤透了元气，还没等他们抽走他们那份‘三分之一’就伤了；而他们抽走的那份‘三分之一’，正是这只烂苹果上唯一好的一块。哼，为我自己的缘故，我并没有向他们讨还什么，所以至少还能省去*你*些麻烦，小乔治。给他们写信，一分钟也别糟蹋；你可千万别指望他们。”

“我不指望，”乔治（George）淡淡地说。“我什么都不指望。”

“哦，咱们还不至于就把事情看成山穷水尽，”乔治·安伯森笑道，只是笑得并不怎么畅快。“咱们总归活得下去，小乔治——*你*尤其活得下去。至于我，年纪大了些，又过惯了伸手靠别人接济的日子，再去跟生活大干一场是提不起来了：能活下去我就知足，而靠一份年薪一千八百美元的领事差事，这点我就办得到。退职的众议员谋这类差事，向来十拿九稳；华盛顿那边传来的话，说事情差不多定了。往后我守着一壶冰水，坐在棕榈树底下，有黑人伺候着——这一半倒像在家里——等安顿下来，我总能隔三差五给你寄上五十美元。我的事就说到这儿！至于你——论自谋生计，你受的那套家教自然差劲得很，可你到底还是个孩子，老门第的那股子根柢在你身上。它总会冒出头来，顶点用处的。那纸契据的事，我永远不肯原谅自己：它本可以给你一笔实实在在的本钱起家。不过，你手头还有一丁点儿，往后还会有一份一丁点儿大的薪水；当然，你的范妮姑妈（Aunt Fanny）也在这儿，她手里有点东西，你*实在*窘极了总还能靠一靠——直到我能开始隔些日子，零星寄一点给你。”

===Original===
George’s “little tiny bit” was six hundred dollars which had come to him from the sale of his mother’s furniture; and the “little tiny salary” was eight dollars a week which old Frank Bronson was to pay him for services as a clerk and student-at-law. Old Frank would have offered more to the Major’s grandson, but since the death of that best of clients and his own experience with automobile headlights, he was not certain of being able to pay more and at the same time settle his own small bills for board and lodging. George had accepted haughtily, and thereby removed a burden from his uncle’s mind.

Amberson himself, however, had not even a “tiny bit”; though he got his consular appointment; and to take him to his post he found it necessary to borrow two hundred of his nephew’s six hundred dollars. “It makes me sick, George,” he said. “But I’d better get there and get that salary started. Of course Eugene would do anything in the world, and the fact is he wanted to, but I felt that—ah—under the circumstances—”

“Never!” George exclaimed, growing red. “I can’t imagine one of the family—” He paused, not finding it necessary to explain that “the family” shouldn’t turn a man from the door and then accept favours from him. “I wish you’d take more.”

Amberson declined. “One thing I’ll say for you, young George; you haven’t a stingy bone in your body. That’s the Amberson stock in you—and I like it!”

===Chinese===
小乔治的那“一丁点儿”，是六百美元，出自变卖他母亲家具的进项；那份“一丁点儿大的薪水”，则是每周八美元，由老弗兰克·布朗森（Frank Bronson）付给他，聘他做文书兼习法学生。老弗兰克原想给少校的外孙多出些；可自从那位顶好的主顾故去，又加上他自己吃汽车车灯的那场惊吓，他不敢确定自己还能多付工钱，同时又清了自己食宿那几笔小账。乔治傲气十足地受了聘，倒也从此卸下了他舅舅心上的一副担子。

不过，乔治·安伯森自己，可是连“一丁点儿”也没有；虽说他到底得了那纸领事任命；要动身赴任，他却发觉不得不向外甥那六百美元里，借去两百。“这让我难受极了，乔治，”他说。“可我总得先到那边，把薪水领起来才行。当然，尤金（Eugene）说什么都肯帮，说实话他也正想帮，只是我觉得——唉——在这种光景之下——”

“万万不能！”乔治涨红了脸嚷道。“我不能想象我们家的人——”他顿住了，觉得犯不上解释：‘我们家’不该把人拒之门外，回头倒又去领人家的情。“我倒希望你多拿些去。”

乔治·安伯森不肯多要。“有你一样，我得说说你，小乔治；你这副身骨里，找不出一根吝啬的骨头。这是你身上安伯森家的血气——我喜欢！”

===Original===
He added something to this praise of his nephew on the day he left for Washington. He was not to return, but to set forth from the capital on the long journey to his post. George went with him to the station, and their farewell was lengthened by the train’s being several minutes late.

“I may not see you again, Georgie,” Amberson said; and his voice was a little husky as he set a kind hand on the young man’s shoulder. “It’s quite probable that from this time on we’ll only know each other by letter—until you’re notified as my next of kin that there’s an old valise to be forwarded to you, and perhaps some dusty curios from the consulate mantelpiece. Well, it’s an odd way for us to be saying goodbye: one wouldn’t have thought it, even a few years ago, but here we are, two gentlemen of elegant appearance in a state of bustitude. We can’t ever tell what will happen at all, can we? Once I stood where we’re standing now, to say goodbye to a pretty girl—only it was in the old station before this was built, and we called it the ‘depot.’ She’d been visiting your mother, before Isabel was married, and I was wild about her, and she admitted she didn’t mind that. In fact, we decided we couldn’t live without each other, and we were to be married. But she had to go abroad first with her father, and when we came to say goodbye we knew we wouldn’t see each other again for almost a year. I thought I couldn’t live through it—and she stood here crying. Well, I don’t even know where she lives now, or if she *is* living—and I only happen to think of her sometimes when I’m here at the station waiting for a train. If she ever thinks of me she probably imagines I’m still dancing in the ballroom at the Amberson Mansion, and she probably thinks of the Mansion as still beautiful—still the finest house in town. Life and money both behave like loose quicksilver in a nest of cracks. And when they’re gone we can’t tell where—or what the devil we did with ’em! But I believe I’ll say now—while there isn’t much time left for either of us to get embarrassed about it—I believe I’ll say that I’ve always been fond of you, Georgie, but I can’t say that I always liked you. Sometimes I’ve felt you were distinctly *not* an acquired taste. Until lately, one had to be fond of you just *naturally*—this isn’t very ‘tactful,’ of course—for if he didn’t, well, he wouldn’t! We all spoiled you terribly when you were a little boy and let you grow up *en prince*—and I must say you took to it! But you’ve received a pretty heavy jolt, and I had enough of your disposition, myself, at your age, to understand a little of what cocksure youth has to go through inside when it finds that it can make terrible mistakes. Poor old fellow! You get both kinds of jolts together, spiritual and material—and you’ve taken them pretty quietly and—well, with my train coming into the shed, you’ll forgive me for saying that there have been times when I thought you ought to be hanged—but I’ve always been fond of you, and now I *like* you! And just for a last word: there may be somebody else in this town who’s always felt about you like that—fond of you, I mean, no matter how much it seemed you ought to be hanged. You might try—Hello, I must run. I’ll send back the money as fast as they pay me—so, goodbye and God bless you, Georgie!”

He passed through the gates, waved his hat cheerily from the other side of the iron screen, and was lost from sight in the hurrying crowd. And as he disappeared, an unexpected poignant loneliness fell upon his nephew so heavily and so suddenly that he had no energy to recoil from the shock. It seemed to him that the last fragment of his familiar world had disappeared, leaving him all alone forever.

===Chinese===
动身去华盛顿那天，他又把夸外甥的这份话添了几句。他这一去便不再回来，而是要从首都直接启程，长途跋涉去赴任。乔治送他到车站；火车误了几分钟，两人的道别倒因此拖长了。

“兴许咱们再见不着了，小乔治，”乔治·安伯森说；他把一只慈爱的手搭上年轻人的肩头，嗓音有点发哑。“打今往后，多半咱们只能靠书信相识了——直到哪一天，有人以我至亲的名义通知你：我有一只旧皮包要寄给你，兴许还有领事馆壁炉台上几件落了灰的小古玩。唉，你我这样道别，实在古怪：搁在几年前，谁也想不到会这样——可眼下，咱们成了两位仪表堂堂的绅士，同陷一种‘破产境’（bustitude）里。往后的祸福，谁也说不准，是不是？当年，就在咱们如今站的地方，我站在这儿，同一位漂亮的姑娘道别——只不过那是在老站里，如今这座还没盖起来，那时大家管它叫‘火车栈’。她来你家做客，住在你母亲那儿——那时伊莎贝尔还没出阁——我为她颠三倒四，她也承认并不嫌这个。说实话，我们认定离了彼此活不成，把婚事都定了。可她得先陪父亲出洋，到了道别那天，我们都知道，这一别差不多要一年才见得着面。我以为自己熬不过这一关——她就站在这儿哭。唉，如今她住在哪儿我都不知道，人*还在不在世*也不知道——只是碰巧在这车站上等车的时候，偶尔还会想起她。她若还偶尔想起我，多半当我还在这安伯森公馆的舞厅里跳舞，多半还当公馆依旧富丽——依旧是全城顶好的宅子。生活跟钱财，都是一摊洒在裂缝窠里的活水银，四处乱滚。等它们滚没了，谁也说不清滚去了哪儿——更见鬼的是，说不清咱们当初究竟拿它们干了什么！可我想，趁着咱们俩还没剩多少工夫来得及害臊，我要说——我一直疼你，小乔治，却不敢说我一直喜欢你。有时我竟觉得，喜欢你这门口味，分明*不是*靠慢慢习惯养成的。直到近来以前，人要疼你，只能*天生*就疼——这话自然说得不大‘圆滑’——因为若非天生就疼，嘿，那便疼不起来！你小时候，咱们把你娇惯得不成样子，任你以*王子做派*长大成人——我也不得不说，你倒真学了个十成！可你如今挨了一记结结实实的闷棍；你那副性子，我自己在你这个年纪也领教得够多，多少明白那种十拿九稳的年轻人，一旦发觉自己也会铸下大错，心里头得熬些什么。可怜的老伙计！你是一记闷棍里挨了两样：精神的一样，身家的一样——而你挨得相当安静，还有——喏，我的车进站棚了，你担待我说一句：有好些回，我当真觉得你该上绞架——可我一直疼你，如今我*喜欢*你了！临了再多一句嘴：这城里兴许还有一个人，对你也始终怀着这份心思——我是说疼你，哪怕再觉得你该上绞架十回。你不妨去试——嗐，我得赶紧了。那边一发薪水，我就把钱给你寄回来——就这样，再见，愿上帝保佑你，小乔治！”

他过了检票口，在铁栅栏那一边兴冲冲地挥着帽子，随即隐没在匆匆的人流里。他一消失，一阵料想不到的刺心孤寂，便重重地、猝然地压上外甥的肩头，压得他连从这一击下退避的气力都没有。在他看来，他熟识的那个世界，最后一块碎片也随之人影杳然，从此把他孤零零地撇下，永生永世。

===Original===
He walked homeward slowly through what appeared to be the strange streets of a strange city; and, as a matter of fact, the city was strange to him. He had seen little of it during his years in college, and then had followed the long absence and his tragic return. Since that he had been “scarcely outdoors at all,” as Fanny complained, warning him that his health would suffer, and he had been downtown only in a closed carriage. He had not realized the great change.

The streets were thunderous; a vast energy heaved under the universal coating of dinginess. George walked through the begrimed crowds of hurrying strangers and saw no face that he remembered. Great numbers of the faces were even of a kind he did not remember ever to have seen; they were partly like the old type that his boyhood knew, and partly like types he knew abroad. He saw German eyes with American wrinkles at their corners; he saw Irish eyes and Neapolitan eyes, Roman eyes, Tuscan eyes, eyes of Lombardy, of Savoy, Hungarian eyes, Balkan eyes, Scandinavian eyes—all with a queer American look in them. He saw Jews who had been German Jews, Jews who had been Russian Jews, Jews who had been Polish Jews but were no longer German or Russian or Polish Jews. All the people were soiled by the smoke-mist through which they hurried, under the heavy sky that hung close upon the new skyscrapers; and nearly all seemed harried by something impending, though here and there a women with bundles would be laughing to a companion about some adventure of the department stores, or perhaps an escape from the charging traffic of the streets—and not infrequently a girl, or a free-and-easy young matron, found time to throw an encouraging look to George.

He took no note of these, and, leaving the crowded sidewalks, turned north into National Avenue, and presently reached the quieter but no less begrimed region of smaller shops and old-fashioned houses. Those latter had been the homes of his boyhood playmates; old friends of his grandfather had lived here;—in this alley he had fought with two boys at the same time, and whipped them; in that front yard he had been successfully teased into temporary insanity by a Sunday-school class of pinky little girls. On that sagging porch a laughing woman had fed him and other boys with doughnuts and gingerbread; yonder he saw the staggered relics of the iron picket fence he had made his white pony jump, on a dare, and in the shabby, stone-faced house behind the fence he had gone to children’s parties, and, when he was a little older he had danced there often, and fallen in love with Mary Sharon, and kissed her, apparently by force, under the stairs in the hall. The double front doors, of meaninglessly carved walnut, once so glossily varnished, had been painted smoke gray, but the smoke grime showed repulsively, even on the smoke gray; and over the doors a smoked sign proclaimed the place to be a “Stag Hotel.”

===Chinese===
他慢慢往家走，穿过的仿佛是一座陌生城里的陌生街巷；而说实在的，这座城市对他也确实陌生。上大学那几年，他在城里就没怎么走动；随后又是长年离乡，再就是那场悲切的归来。打那以后，用范妮抱怨的话说，他“简直不出门”，她还警告他这样要弄坏身体的；他进城，也只坐闭式马车。天翻地覆的变化，他竟没有察觉。

街上雷声隆隆；一片无边的灰垢底下，有一股巨大的力量在起伏奔涌。乔治从满身污垢、行色匆匆的陌生人群里穿过，见不着一张记得的脸。许多面孔，甚至是种他不记得曾见过的类型：一半像他童年熟识的旧式人，一半像他在国外见过的各色人等。他看见德国人的眼睛，眼角带着美国式的皱纹；他看见爱尔兰人的眼睛、那不勒斯人的眼睛、罗马人的眼睛、托斯卡纳人的眼睛，伦巴第的、萨伏依的，匈牙利人的、巴尔干的、斯堪的纳维亚的眼睛——眼珠里都带着一股古怪的美国神情。他看见犹太人：原本是德国犹太的，原本是俄国犹太的，原本是波兰犹太的——如今既不是德国的，也不是俄国的，也不是波兰的了。人人都在烟尘迷雾里奔忙，被熏得灰头土脸；头顶沉甸甸的天，低低压着新起的摩天楼；而几乎人人脸上都像有什么大祸临头，只是东一处西一处，也有抱着大包小包的女人，正跟同伴笑谈百货公司里的什么历险，或是笑谈方才如何死里逃生，躲过了街上横冲直撞的车流——还不时有个姑娘，或是位不拘小节的年轻太太，腾出工夫朝乔治投来鼓励的一瞥。

这些他一概没有留意。他离开拥挤的人行道，折向北，进了国民大道（National Avenue），不多时便到了一片清静些、却同样污黑的地带：小铺子，老式住房。后者曾是他童年玩伴的家；他外祖父的老朋友也住过这一带——就在这条巷子里，他一个人同两个男孩对打，还把他们揍赢了；在那家前院里，主日学校一群粉扑扑的小姑娘，把他撩拨得一时发昏，大失常态。那道下陷的门廊上，一位爱笑的女人曾拿炸面圈和姜饼，喂过他跟别的男孩；那边，他望见一座铁尖桩栅栏的残骸歪歪斜斜——当年有人激他，他让自己的小白马从上头跳了过去；栅栏后头那座石面的破房子里，他赴过多少孩子们的茶会；再大些，他又常在那里跳舞，爱上了玛丽·莎伦（Mary Sharon），还在门厅楼梯底下吻了她——看那情形，多半是硬来的。两扇雕着莫名花纹的胡桃木双开大门，从前漆得那样光可鉴人，如今刷成了烟灰色，可烟垢污迹却依旧丑陋地透出来，连烟灰色也遮它不住；门楣上方一块熏黑的招牌，宣告此处是一家“单身汉旅馆”（Stag Hotel）。

===Original===
Other houses had become boardinghouses too genteel for signs, but many were franker, some offering “board by the day, week or meal,” and some, more laconic, contenting themselves with the label: “Rooms.” One, having torn out part of an old stone-trimmed bay window for purposes of commercial display, showed forth two suspended petticoats and a pair of oyster-coloured flannel trousers to prove the claims of its black-and-gilt sign: “French Cleaning and Dye House.” Its next neighbour also sported a remodelled front and permitted no doubt that its mission in life was to attend cosily upon death: “J. M. Rolsener. Caskets. The Funeral Home.” And beyond that, a plain old honest foursquare gray-painted brick house was flamboyantly decorated with a great gilt scroll on the railing of the old-fashioned veranda: “Mutual Benev’t Order Cavaliers and Dames of Purity.” This was the old Minafer house.

George passed it without perceptibly wincing; in fact, he held his head up, and except for his gravity of countenance and the prison pallor he had acquired by too constantly remaining indoors, there was little to warn an acquaintance that he was not precisely the same George Amberson Minafer known aforetime. He was still so magnificent, indeed, that there came to his ears a waft of comment from a passing automobile. This was a fearsome red car, glittering in brass, with half-a-dozen young people in it whose motorism had reached an extreme manifestation in dress. The ladies of this party were favourably affected at sight of the pedestrian upon the sidewalk, and, as the machine was moving slowly, and close to the curb, they had time to observe him in detail, which they did with a frankness not pleasing to the object of their attentions. “One sees so many nice-looking people one doesn’t know nowadays,” said the youngest of the young ladies. “This old town of ours is really getting enormous. I shouldn’t mind knowing who he is.”

“*I* don’t know,” the youth beside her said, loudly enough to be heard at a considerable distance. “I don’t know who he is, but from his looks I know who he thinks he is: he thinks he’s the Grand Duke Cuthbert!” There was a burst of tittering as the car gathered speed and rolled away, with the girl continuing to look back until her scandalized companions forced her to turn by pulling her hood over her face. She made an impression upon George, so deep a one, in fact, that he unconsciously put his emotion into a muttered word:

“Riffraff!”

===Chinese===
另一些房子做了包饭公寓，体面得不肯挂招牌；可更多的直来直去，有的招揽“按日、按周、按顿供膳”，有的更省话，只消一块字牌：“出租房间”。有一家，为着做买卖，把一座旧日镶石边的凸窗拆去了一半，从窗洞里吊出两条衬裙、一条牡蛎色的法兰绒裤子，来替它那黑底描金的招牌作证：“法式洗染行”。紧挨着的邻家，也把门面翻新了一道，让人对自己的营生一望而知——那营生，便是舒舒服服地伺候死亡：“J. M. 罗尔塞纳。灵柩。殡仪馆”。再过去，是一座四四方方、灰砖灰皮的朴实老房子，却张扬地在老式阳台的栏杆上挂了大幅鎏金卷轴：“纯洁骑士淑女互济会”。这便是米纳弗家的老宅。

乔治打它门前走过，脸上瞧不出一丝痉挛；实则他把头昂得很高，只是面色凝重，又添了久居室内落下的囚徒似的苍白——除此之外，熟人也难看出，他已不是从前那个乔治·安伯森·米纳弗（George Amberson Minafer）了。他仍旧气派得很，气派到一辆驶过的汽车里飘来几句议论，落进了他的耳朵。那是一辆骇人的红汽车，满身铜件锃亮，里头坐着六七个年轻人，一身的汽车习气（motorism），连衣着都表露到了极端。这一伙里的女眷们，一瞥见人行道上的行人，便顿生好感；车又开得慢，又贴着路缘，正好容她们把这个人细细端详一番，端详得又那样坦率，全不顾被端详的人受不受用。“如今这城里，不认得的漂亮人儿也见着好多啦，”小姐们里最年轻的那位说。“咱们这座老城，真要大得没边了。我还真想知道他是谁。”

“*我*可不知道，”她身边的青年说，嗓门大得隔着老远都听得见。“我不知道他是谁，可瞧他那模样，我知道他当自己是谁：他当自己是库斯伯特大公（Grand Duke Cuthbert）！”车加快速度，辘辘滚远了，车里爆出一阵吃吃的笑；那姑娘还在回头看，直到同车的伴侣又惊又臊，把风帽拉下来蒙住她的脸，才逼她转过头去。她给乔治留下了印象——实在是极深的一道，深得他不知不觉，把这一腔激荡化作了一声嘀咕：

“下三烂！”

===Original===
This was the last “walk home” he was ever to take by the route he was now following: up National Avenue to Amberson Addition and the two big old houses at the foot of Amberson Boulevard; for tonight would be the last night that he and Fanny were to spend in the house which the Major had forgotten to deed to Isabel. Tomorrow they were to “move out,” and George was to begin his work in Bronson’s office. He had not come to this collapse without a fierce struggle—but the struggle was inward, and the rolling world was not agitated by it, and rolled calmly on. For of all the “ideals of life” which the world, in its rolling, inconsiderately flattens out to nothingness, the least likely to retain a profile is that ideal which depends upon inheriting money. George Amberson, in spite of his record of failures in business, had spoken shrewdly when he realized at last that money, like life, was “like quicksilver in a nest of cracks.” And his nephew had the awakening experience of seeing the great Amberson Estate vanishing into such a nest—in a twinkling, it seemed, now that it was indeed so utterly vanished.

His uncle had suggested that he might write to college friends; perhaps they could help him to something better than the prospect offered by Bronson’s office; but George flushed and shook his head, without explaining. In that small and quietly superior “crowd” of his he had too emphatically supported the ideal of being rather than doing. He could not appeal to one of its members now to help him to a job. Besides, they were not precisely the warmest-hearted crew in the world, and he had long ago dropped the last affectation of a correspondence with any of them. He was as aloof from any survival of intimacy with his boyhood friends in the city, and, in truth, had lost track of most of them. “The Friends of the Ace,” once bound by oath to succour one another in peril or poverty, were long ago dispersed; one or two had died; one or two had gone to live elsewhere; the others were disappeared into the smoky bigness of the heavy city. Of the brethren, there remained within his present cognizance only his old enemy, the red-haired Kinney, now married to Janie Sharon, and Charlie Johnson, who, out of deference to his mother’s memory, had passed the Amberson Mansion one day, when George stood upon the front steps, and, looking in fiercely, had looked away with continued fierceness—his only token of recognition.

… On this last homeward walk of his, when George reached the entrance to Amberson Addition—that is, when he came to where the entrance had formerly been—he gave a little start, and halted for a moment to stare. This was the first time he had noticed that the stone pillars, marking the entrance, had been removed. Then he realized that for a long time he had been conscious of a queerness about this corner without being aware of what made the difference. National Avenue met Amberson Boulevard here at an obtuse angle, and the removal of the pillars made the Boulevard seem a cross-street of no overpowering importance—certainly it did not seem to be a boulevard!

===Chinese===
这条他此刻正走的回家路，他是再也不走了：沿国民大道上行，到安伯森新城区（Amberson Addition），到安伯森大道（Amberson Boulevard）脚下那两座大老宅；因为今晚，是他和范妮在那座少校忘了立契给伊莎贝尔的房子里，过的最后一夜。明天，他们就要“搬出去”，乔治也要到布朗森的事务所开始做事。他不是没有经过一番死命的挣扎，才落到这步田地——只是挣扎在心里，滚滚向前的世界并不为它所动，依旧平平静静地滚。须知世上种种“人生理想”，任世界滚滚向前、不管不顾地碾作乌有，其中最保不住自己轮廓的，便是那仰仗继承钱财的一种。乔治·安伯森虽然生意场上败绩累累，这一回却说话在理：他到头来省悟到，钱同生活一样，“就像裂缝窠里的水银”。而他的外甥，也领受了那一记叫人猛醒的经验：眼看着庞大的安伯森产业，滚进了这样一窝裂缝——转眼间，似乎真就是转眼间，它已消失得干干净净，一丝不剩。

舅舅提议，他不妨写信给大学里的朋友，兴许他们能替他谋个比布朗森事务所前景更好的出路；乔治却红了脸摇头，并不解释。在他那个小小的、不动声色地自视高人一等的“圈子”里，他曾太起劲地拥护过“做人”高于“做事”的理想；如今他不能反过来，求圈子里哪一位替他谋个差事。何况那一伙人，心肠也说不上是世上最热的一等；同他们任何一位书信往来的最后一点装模作样，他早已撂下了。城中童年朋友的旧谊，凡有存余的，他也一概疏远；说实在的，大半的人，连音信都断了。“爱司之友会”（the “Friends of the Ace”）当年誓同患难、贫贱相扶，早已风流云散：一两个死了，一两个迁去了别处，其余的都消失在这座沉重城市烟腾腾的庞大躯体里。弟兄们当中，如今还在他耳目之内的，只剩他的老冤家红头发的金尼（Kinney）——如今已娶了珍妮·莎伦（Janie Sharon）——和查理·约翰逊（Charlie Johnson）；约翰逊出于对亡母的敬念，有一天打安伯森公馆门前走过，那天乔治正站在门前台阶上；他狠狠地朝里望了一眼，又带着未消的凶相把目光挪开——这便是他认得这位故人的唯一表示。

……他这最后一次步行回家，走到安伯森新城区的入口——就是说，走到入口从前所在的地方——不由得微微一怔，停下脚盯了半晌。直到这时他才头一回留意到，标示入口的那两根石柱，已经拆走了。他这才明白，这街角好长一阵子在他眼里总觉得有点不对劲，却又说不清差别何在。国民大道在这里斜斜地交上安伯森大道，成一个钝角；石柱一拆，那条大道看着便像一条无足轻重的横街——说它是条大道，任谁也看不出来了！

===Original===
At the next corner Neptune’s Fountain remained, and one could still determine with accuracy what its designer’s intentions had been. It stood in sore need of just one last kindness; and if the thing had possessed any friends they would have done that doleful shovelling after dark.

George did not let his eyes linger upon the relic; nor did he look steadfastly at the Amberson Mansion. Massive as the old house was, it managed to look gaunt: its windows stared with the skull emptiness of all windows in empty houses that are to be lived in no more. Of course the rowdy boys of the neighbourhood had been at work: many of these haggard windows were broken; the front door stood ajar, forced open; and idiot salacity, in white chalk, was smeared everywhere upon the pillars and stonework of the verandas.

George walked by the Mansion hurriedly, and came home to his mother’s house for the last time.

===Chinese===
隔一个街角，尼普顿喷泉（Neptune’s Fountain）还在，设计者当初的意图，如今仍能考据得分毫不差。它如今只缺这最后一份慈悲了；这物件若也算有几个朋友，他们自会趁着天黑下手，铲土把它葬了。

乔治没有让自己的眼睛在那残骸上流连；安伯森公馆，他也没有死死地看。那老宅虽然体量庞大，却硬是显出一副嶙峋相：一扇扇窗空空地瞪着，跟一切不再住人的空房子一样，瞪出骷髅似的空洞。左近那些顽童自然早来光顾过了：憔悴的窗破了许多扇；前门半开半掩，是撬开的；白粉笔涂的下流浑话，爬满了阳台的柱子和石作，到处都是。

乔治匆匆走过公馆，最后一次回到母亲的那所房子。

===Original===
Emptiness was there, too, and the closing of the door resounded through bare rooms; for downstairs there was no furniture in the house except a kitchen table in the dining room, which Fanny had kept “for dinner,” she said, though as she was to cook and serve that meal herself George had his doubts about her name for it. Upstairs, she had retained her own furniture, and George had been living in his mother’s room, having sent everything from his own to the auction. Isabel’s room was still as it had been, but the furniture would be moved with Fanny’s to new quarters in the morning. Fanny had made plans for her nephew as well as herself; she had found a three-room “kitchenette apartment” in an apartment house where several old friends of hers had established themselves—elderly widows of citizens once “prominent” and other retired gentry. People used their own “kitchenettes” for breakfast and lunch, but there was a table-d’hôte arrangement for dinner on the ground floor; and after dinner bridge was played all evening, an attraction powerful with Fanny. She had “made all the arrangements,” she reported, and nervously appealed for approval, asking if she hadn’t shown herself “pretty practical” in such matters. George acquiesced absentmindedly, not thinking of what she said and not realizing to what it committed him.

He began to realize it now, as he wandered about the dismantled house; he was far from sure that he was willing to go and live in a “three-room apartment” with Fanny and eat breakfast and lunch with her (prepared by herself in the “kitchenette”) and dinner at the table-d’hôte in “such a pretty Colonial dining room” (so Fanny described it) at a little round table they would have all to themselves in the midst of a dozen little round tables which other relics of disrupted families would have all to themselves. For the first time, now that the change was imminent, George began to develop before his mind’s eye pictures of what he was in for; and they appalled him. He decided that such a life verged upon the sheerly unbearable, and that after all there were some things left that he just couldn’t stand. So he made up his mind to speak to his aunt about it at “dinner,” and tell her that he preferred to ask Bronson to let him put a sofa-bed, a trunk, and a folding rubber bathtub behind a screen in the dark rear room of the office. George felt that this would be infinitely more tolerable; and he could eat at restaurants, especially as about all he ever wanted nowadays was coffee.

But at “dinner” he decided to put off telling Fanny of his plan until later: she was so nervous, and so distressed about the failure of her efforts with sweetbreads and macaroni; and she was so eager in her talk of how comfortable they would be “by this time tomorrow night.” She fluttered on, her nervousness increasing, saying how “nice” it would be for him, when he came from work in the evenings, to be among “nice people—people who know who we *are*,” and to have a pleasant game of bridge with “people who are really old friends of the family.”

===Chinese===
空虚也在那里等着；门一关上，响声在四壁空空的屋子里回荡。楼下的家具，只剩饭厅里一张厨房用的桌子——范妮留着它“吃正餐”，她这么说；可这顿饭要她自己下厨、自己端上，乔治对她给它起的这个名目，未免存疑。楼上，她留下了自己那套家具；乔治呢，自己的东西全送去拍卖了，一直住在母亲的房里。伊莎贝尔的房间还照原样，只是那套家具，明早要连同范妮的一起，搬往新的住处。范妮不但替自己作了安排，也替外甥作了安排：她在一家公寓楼里寻着一处三室的“小厨房公寓”（kitchenette apartment），她几位老朋友就在那里安了家——都是些一度“知名”的市民留下的年迈遗孀，和别的退休闲人。各家的小厨房，自做早饭午饭；楼下一层备着一顿包饭的晚餐；饭后打桥牌，一打一晚上——这一条，对范妮自有莫大的魔力。她“全安排妥了”，她这样禀报，又惴惴地讨一句嘉许，问她这些事上算不算显出了“很会当家”。乔治心不在焉地应着，没去想她说了什么，也没想到这一应，把自己应进了什么。

这会儿，他在拆空了的宅子里踱来踱去，才开始回过味来。同范妮住进一处“三室的公寓”，早饭午饭跟她一道吃（由她本人在“小厨房”里做），晚饭吃包饭——在“那么雅致的一间殖民地式饭厅”（范妮的说法）里，占一张小圆桌，一张单归他们俩的小圆桌，四周环着十几张小圆桌，各归各的那些破落人家的遗老遗少，也是单占的——这一层他肯不肯，他自己远没有把握。变化迫在眉睫，乔治这才头一回，把自己要过的是什么日子，一幅一幅在心里描画出来；这一描画，把他吓着了。他断定这样的日子简直没法忍受，到底世上还剩下几样东西，是他万万受不住的。于是他打定主意，“正餐”桌上跟姑妈提，说他宁可求布朗森行个方便，容他把一张沙发床、一口箱子和一只折叠的橡皮浴盆，安置在事务所后面那间黑屋子里，隔一道屏风。乔治觉得，那要舒坦得多；饭可以上馆子吃，何况如今他想要的，差不多也就是一杯咖啡了。

可到了“正餐”桌上，他改了主意，打算把这套办法往后放一放再说：她那么心神不宁，又为小牛胰和通心粉都做了砸了而那么伤心；谈起“明儿这个时辰”他们会过得多舒服，她又谈得那么起劲。她絮絮地扑腾着，神经越发绷得紧，说晚上他下了班回去，身边围着“上等人——知道我们*是谁*的人”，那该有多“好”；又能同“真正是家里的老朋友”的人们，痛痛快快打几局桥牌。

===Original===
When they stopped probing among the scorched fragments she had set forth, George lingered downstairs, waiting for a better opportunity to introduce his own subject, but when he heard dismaying sounds from the kitchen he gave up. There was a crash, then a shower of crashes; falling tin clamoured to be heard above the shattering of porcelain; and over all rose Fanny’s wail of lamentation for the treasures saved from the sale, but now lost forever to the “kitchenette.” Fanny was nervous indeed; so nervous that she could not trust her hands.

For a moment George thought she might have been injured, but, before he reached the kitchen, he heard her sweeping at the fragments, and turned back. He put off speaking to Fanny until morning.

Things more insistent than his vague plans for a sofa-bed in Bronson’s office had possession of his mind as he went upstairs, moving his hand slowly along the smooth walnut railing of the balustrade. Halfway to the landing he stopped, turned, and stood looking down at the heavy doors masking the black emptiness that had been the library. Here he had stood on what he now knew was the worst day of his life; here he had stood when his mother passed through that doorway, hand-in-hand with her brother, to learn what her son had done.

He went on more heavily, more slowly; and, more heavily and slowly still, entered Isabel’s room and shut the door. He did not come forth again, and bade Fanny good night through the closed door when she stopped outside it later.

===Chinese===
两人把她摆出来的那些烤糊的碎块拨拉完了，乔治还赖在楼下，想等个更好的时机提出自己的事；可厨房里传来叫人心惊的声响，他便断了念想。先是一声巨响，跟着哗啦啦下起一阵碎响的雨；铁皮家什砸下来，铛铛地吵着要盖过瓷器粉碎的声音；而压倒一切的，是范妮嚎啕的哀哭——哭那些从拍卖场上抢救下来的宝贝，如今同“小厨房”永远断了缘份。范妮是真慌了神；慌得连自己的手都不敢信了。

一时之间，乔治还当砸着了她；没等走到厨房，他听见她已在扫那些碎渣，便转身退了回来。跟范妮谈的事，他推到了明天早上。

他上楼去，一只手顺着楼梯光溜溜的胡桃木栏杆慢慢抚上去；这时，盘踞在他心头的，已不是在布朗森事务所里安一张沙发床的那点模糊打算，而是更揪心的事了。离楼梯平台还有一半，他站住，回过身，朝下望着那两扇沉甸甸的门——门后挡着一片黑洞洞的空，从前那里是图书室。如今他知道了，那是他一生最坏的一天，他就站在这里；他站在这里，望着母亲牵着哥哥的手，穿过那道门，去听她儿子闯下的祸。

他的脚步愈发沉重，愈发迟缓；又愈发沉重、愈发迟缓地，进了伊莎贝尔的房间，关上了门。他没有再出来；后来范妮在门外停了停，他隔着关死的门，向她道了晚安。

===Original===
“I’ve put all the lights out, George,” she said. “Everything’s all right.”

“Very well,” he called. “Good night.”

She did not go. “I’m sure we’re going to enjoy the new little home, George,” she said timidly. “I’ll try hard to make things nice for you, and the people really are lovely. You mustn’t feel as if things are altogether gloomy, George. I know everything’s going to turn out all right. You’re young and strong and you have a good mind and I’m sure—” she hesitated—“I’m sure your mother’s watching over you, Georgie. Good night, dear.”

“Good night, Aunt Fanny.”

===Chinese===
“灯我全熄了，乔治，”她说。“都好好的，没事了。”

“好，”他应道。“晚安。”

她没有走。“咱们那个新的小家，一定过得舒坦，我有数，乔治，”她怯生生地说。“我会想法子把什么都给你弄得妥妥帖帖，那些人也实在是可亲。你千万别觉得事事都灰暗，乔治。我知道样样都会好起来的。你年轻，身子结实，脑子又好使，我敢保——”她迟疑了，“我敢保，你妈妈正照看着你呢，小乔治。晚安，亲爱的。”

“晚安，范妮姑妈。”

===Original===
His voice had a strangled sound in spite of him; but she seemed not to notice it, and he heard her go to her own room and lock herself in with bolt and key against burglars. She had said the one thing she should not have said just then: “I’m sure your mother’s watching over you, Georgie.” She had meant to be kind, but it destroyed his last chance for sleep that night. He would have slept little if she had not said it, but since she had said it, he could not sleep at all. For he knew that it was true—if it *could* be true—and that his mother, if she still lived in spirit, would be weeping on the other side of the wall of silence, weeping and seeking for some gate to let her through so that she could come and “watch over him.”

He felt that if there were such gates they were surely barred: they were like those awful library doors downstairs, which had shut her in to begin the suffering to which he had consigned her.

The room was still Isabel’s. Nothing had been changed: even the photographs of George, of the Major, and of “brother George” still stood on her dressing-table, and in a drawer of her desk was an old picture of Eugene and Lucy, taken together, which George had found, but had slowly closed away again from sight, not touching it. Tomorrow everything would be gone; and he had heard there was not long to wait before the house itself would be demolished. The very space which tonight was still Isabel’s room would be cut into new shapes by new walls and floors and ceilings; yet the room would always live, for it could not die out of George’s memory. It would live as long as he did, and it would always be murmurous with a tragic, wistful whispering.

===Chinese===
尽管他自己极力忍着，那嗓音里还是带出一声哽噎；可她似乎没有留意，他听见她回了自己房里，落闩上锁，把自己关在里头防贼。她偏偏说了那么一句，恰恰是这时候最不该说的话：“我敢保，你妈妈正照看着你呢，小乔治。”她原是一片好意，可这一句，把他今夜睡的最后一星指望也砸了。她不说，他本来也睡不了多少；她说了，他竟完全睡不着了。因为他知道这话是真的——倘若这话*能够*是真的——他知道，母亲倘若精灵犹在，一定在沉默之墙的那一边哭，一面哭，一面寻着一道门，好让她穿过来，来“照看他”。

他觉得，即便真有那样的门，也一定闩死了：那就像楼下图书室那两扇可怕的门，当初正是它们把她关了进去，开始受那份罪——那份由他一手加给她的罪。

这房间依旧是伊莎贝尔的。什么都没有动过：连那张乔治的相片、少校的相片、“乔治哥哥”的相片，还立在梳妆台上；书桌抽屉里，有一张尤金和露西的旧合影，乔治翻到过，又慢慢阖上，收回视线之外，没有碰它。明天，一切都要搬空了；而且他听说，要不了多久，连这所房子本身也要拆掉。今夜还是伊莎贝尔房间的这方空间，就要让新的墙、新的地板、新的顶棚，裁成新的模样；然而这房间会永远活下去，因为它死不出乔治的记忆。他活多久，它便活多久，而且永远低回着一种悲切的、怅惘的絮语。

===Original===
And if space itself can be haunted, as memory is haunted, then some time, when the space that was Isabel’s room came to be made into the small bedrooms and “kitchenettes” already designed as its destiny, that space might well be haunted and the new occupants come to feel that some seemingly causeless depression hung about it—a wraith of the passion that filled it throughout the last night that George Minafer spent there.

Whatever remnants of the old high-handed arrogance were still within him, he did penance for his deepest sin that night—and it may be that to this day some impressionable, overworked woman in a “kitchenette,” after turning out the light will seem to see a young man kneeling in the darkness, shaking convulsively, and, with arms outstretched through the wall, clutching at the covers of a shadowy bed. It may seem to her that she hears the faint cry, over and over:

“Mother, forgive me! *God*, forgive me!”

===Chinese===
倘若空间也同记忆一样会闹鬼，那么有一天，待伊莎贝尔房间的这方空间，照它注定的命运，改作一间间小卧室和一间间“小厨房”的时候，这方空间多半当真有鬼；新住进来的人，说不定会觉出一种说不出来由的郁沉沉，总在这一带盘桓——那是充塞此屋的满腔深情留下的魂影，遗自乔治·米纳弗在此度过的最后一夜，那一整夜。

他身上旧日的专横傲慢纵有残余，那一夜，也在为他最深重的罪过补赎；兴许直到今天，哪一位多感的、操劳过度的女人，在一间“小厨房”里熄了灯，还仿佛看见黑暗里跪着个年轻人，浑身痉挛地抖，两条胳膊穿过墙伸出去，死死攥着一张影影绰绰的床上的被衾。她兴许还听得见那声细弱的呼喊，一遍又一遍：

“妈妈，饶恕我！*上帝*，饶恕我！”

## XXXII / 三十二

===Original===
At least, it may be claimed for George that his last night in the house where he had been born was not occupied with his own disheartening future, but with sorrow for what sacrifices his pride and youth had demanded of others. And early in the morning he came downstairs and tried to help Fanny make coffee on the kitchen range.

“There was something I wanted to say to you last night, Aunt Fanny,” he said, as she finally discovered that an amber fluid, more like tea than coffee, was as near ready to be taken into the human system as it would ever be. “I think I’d better do it now.”

She set the coffeepot back upon the stove with a little crash, and, looking at him in a desperate anxiety, began to twist her dainty apron between her fingers without any consciousness of what she was doing.

===Chinese===
至少，有一点尽可以替乔治·安伯森·米纳弗（George Amberson Minafer）争一争：他在这座生身老宅的最后一夜，占据他心头的，并不是他自己那令人气馁的前程，而是一种哀伤——哀伤他的骄傲与年少逼得旁人付出了怎样的牺牲。一大清早，他便下楼来，试着帮范妮在灶上煮咖啡。

“范妮姑妈，昨儿夜里我就有句话要对你说，”他说。这工夫，她终于认定：这泓琥珀色的液体——说像茶总比像咖啡多些——要送进人的身体，如今的火候已是它此生所能有的极致了。“我想，顶好这会儿就说。”

她把咖啡壶往灶上一搁，磕出轻轻的一响；接着惶惶然死死望着他，手指间无意识地绞起那条精巧的小围裙来，自己全然不觉。

===Original===
“Why—why—” she stammered; but she knew what he was going to say, and that was why she had been more and more nervous. “Hadn’t—perhaps—perhaps we’d better get the—the things moved to the little new home first, George. Let’s—”

He interrupted quietly, though at her phrase, “the little new home,” his pungent impulse was to utter one loud shout and run. “It was about this new place that I wanted to speak. I’ve been thinking it over, and I’ve decided. I want you to take all the things from mother’s room and use them and keep them for me, and I’m sure the little apartment will be just what you like; and with the extra bedroom probably you could find some woman friend to come and live there, and share the expense with you. But I’ve decided on another arrangement for myself, and so I’m not going with you. I don’t suppose you’ll mind much, and I don’t see why you *should* mind—particularly, that is. I’m not very lively company these days, or any days, for that matter. I can’t imagine you, or anyone else, being much attached to me, so—”

He stopped in amazement: no chair had been left in the kitchen, but Fanny gave a despairing glance around her, in search of one, then sank abruptly, and sat flat upon the floor.

===Chinese===
“呃——呃——”她嗫嚅着；可她知道他要说什么，这也正是她近来愈来愈心神不定的缘故。“要不——兴许——兴许咱们顶好先把那些——那些东西搬到小小的新家去，乔治。咱们先——”

他静静地截住了她的话头；只是听到她那句‘小小的新家’，一阵剧烈的冲动攫住了他，只想放声喊上一嗓子，撒腿就跑。“我要说的，正是这个新住处。我来来回回想过了，主意已定。母亲房里的那些东西，我要你全数拿去用，替我收存着；那套小公寓，我信一定正合你的心意；多出来的那间卧室，兴许你还能邀一位女朋友来同住，跟你合摊开销。至于我自己，我另有了安排，不跟你去了。我想你未必怎么在意，我也看不出你*凭什么*要在意——我是说，特别在意。眼下这几天，不论哪一天，我都算不得什么叫人快活的伴儿。我想不出你，或是随便哪个旁人，会对我怀有多深的情分，所以——”

他愕然收住了话：厨房里一把椅子也没剩下，范妮却绝望地朝四下里扫了一眼，像在寻一把椅子，随即猛地往下一沉，一屁股坐平在了地板上。

===Original===
“You’re going to leave me in the lurch!” she gasped.

“What on earth—” George sprang to her. “Get up, Aunt Fanny!”

“I can’t. I’m too weak. Let me alone, George!” And as he released the wrist he had seized to help her, she repeated the dismal prophecy which for days she had been matching against her hopes: “You’re going to leave me—in the lurch!”

===Chinese===
“你要撇下我不管了！”她倒抽着气说。

“这到底——”乔治一步蹿到她跟前。“起来，范妮姑妈！”

“起不来。我浑身没得力气。放开我，乔治！”待他松开方才为扶她而攥住的那只手腕，她又把那不祥的谶语念了一遍——连日来，她一直拿这句话同自己的指望暗暗较量：“你要撇下我——不管了！”

===Original===
“Why no, Aunt Fanny!” he protested. “At first I’d have been something of a burden on you. I’m to get eight dollars a week; about thirty-two a month. The rent’s thirty-six dollars a month, and the table-d’hôte dinner runs up to over twenty-two dollars apiece, so with my half of the rent—eighteen dollars—I’d have less than nothing left out of my salary to pay my share of the groceries for all the breakfasts and luncheons. You see you’d not only be doing all the housework and cooking, but you’d be paying more of the expenses than I would.”

She stared at him with such a forlorn blankness as he had never seen. “I’d be paying—” she said feebly. “I’d be paying—”

“Certainly you would. You’d be using more of your money than—”

===Chinese===
“哪儿能呢，范妮姑妈！”他分辩道。“起头儿那些日子，我多少要算你的一副担子。我每周挣八块钱，一个月三十二块上下。房租每月三十六块；包桌客饭一人一餐就要二十多块；这么一来，我那半份房租——十八块——一扣，薪水剩下的连个零头都不够，早饭午饭种种杂货我该摊的那一份，更没了着落。你瞧，这么着，你不但家务、厨下一肩全挑，掏的钱还得比我的多。”

她直瞪瞪地望着他，那副凄惶茫然的呆相，他生平未见。“我要掏——”她有气无力地说。“我要掏——”

“自然要掏。你花的自己的钱比——”

===Original===
“My money!” Fanny’s chin drooped upon her thin chest, and she laughed miserably. “I’ve got twenty-eight dollars. That’s all.”

“You mean until the interest is due again?”

“I mean that’s all,” Fanny said. “I mean that’s all there is. There won’t be any more interest because there isn’t any principal.”

===Chinese===
“我的钱！”范妮的下巴垂落到瘦削的胸口上，惨然一笑。“我手里统共二十八块钱。就这些。”

“你是说，到下回利息到期为止？”

“我是说就这些，”范妮说。“我是说，全部家当就这些。不会再有利息了，因为本金已经没有了。”

===Original===
“Why, you told—”

She shook her head. “No, I haven’t told you anything.”

“Then it was Uncle George. *He* told me you had enough to fall back on. That’s just what he said: ‘to fall back on.’ He said you’d lost more than you should, in the headlight company, but he’d insisted that you should hold out enough to live on, and you’d very wisely followed his advice.”

===Chinese===
“咦，你说起过——”

她摇了摇头。“没有，我什么也没跟你说过。”

“那准是乔治舅舅。*是*他告诉我，你手头留足了退路。原话就是他那个说法：‘留作退路。’他说你在头灯公司里蚀得过了分，可他坚主你该留出足以度日的底子，而你也十分精明地依了他的主意。”

===Original===
“I know,” she said weakly. “I told him so. He didn’t know, or else he’d forgotten, how much Wilbur’s insurance amounted to, and I—oh, it seemed such a sure way to make a real fortune out of a little—and I thought I could do something for *you*, George, if you ever came to need it—and it all looked so bright I just thought I’d put it all in. I did—every cent except my last interest payment—and it’s gone.”

“Good Lord!” George began to pace up and down on the worn planks of the bare floor. “Why on earth did you wait till now to tell such a thing as this?”

“I *couldn’t* tell till I had to,” she said piteously. “I couldn’t till George Amberson went away. He couldn’t do anything to help, anyhow, and I just didn’t want him to talk to me about it—he’s been at me so much about not putting more in than I could afford to lose, and said he considered he had my—my word I *wasn’t* putting more than that in it. So I thought: What was the use? What was the use of going over it all with him and having him reproach me, and probably reproach himself? It wouldn’t do any good—not any good on earth.” She got out her lace handkerchief and began to cry. “Nothing does any good, I guess, in this old world. Oh, how tired of this old world I am! I didn’t know *what* to do. I just tried to go ahead and be as practical as I could, and arrange *some* way for us to live. Oh, I knew you didn’t want me, George! You always teased me and berated me whenever you had a chance from the time you were a little boy—you did *so*! Later, you’ve tried to be kinder to me, but you don’t want me around—oh, I can see *that* much! You don’t suppose I want to thrust myself on you, do you? It isn’t very pleasant to be thrusting yourself on a person you know doesn’t want you—but I knew you oughtn’t to be left all alone in the world; it isn’t good. I knew your mother’d want me to watch over you and try to have *something* like a home for you—I know she’d want me to do what I tried to do!” Fanny’s tears were bitter now, and her voice, hoarse and wet, was tragically sincere. “I tried—I tried to be practical—to look after your interests—to make things as nice for you as I could—I walked my heels down looking for a place for us to live—I walked and walked over this town—I didn’t ride one block on a streetcar—I wouldn’t use five cents no matter how tired I—Oh!” She sobbed uncontrollably. “Oh! and now—you don’t want—you want—you want to leave me in the lurch! You—”

===Chinese===
“我知道，”她虚弱地说。“我对他也是这么说的。威尔伯的保险金一共有多少，他不知道，要不就是忘了；而我——哦，那看着实在是一条十拿九稳的路子，能把小钱滚成真正的家当——我还想着，乔治，万一哪天*你*用得着，我也好为你派上用场——眼前一片光明，我就索性想，索性全放进去吧。我真放了——除去末一回的利息，一个子儿不留——如今全蚀光了。”

“我的老天爷！”乔治在光秃秃的地板上，踩着磨旧的木板来回踱起步来。“这样的事，你干吗偏挨到今天才说？”

“不挨到非说不可的时候，我*开不了口*，”她凄凄惶惶地说。“乔治·安伯森舅舅没走，我开不了口。反正他也帮不上什么忙，我就是不愿听他为这件事来同我讲——为着‘放进去的钱别超过赔得起的数’，他念叨过我多少回，还说照他看，他手里有我的——我的许诺：我*并没*放进超过那个数的钱。于是我就想：有什么用呢？同他从头到尾翻腾一遍，惹他责备我，兴许还惹他责备他自己，有什么用呢？一点用处也没有——天底下一点用处也没有。”她掏出花边手帕，哭了起来。“我猜，在这个老世界上，做什么都不顶用。哦，这个老世界，我真是腻味透了！我不知道*怎么办*才好。我只顾硬着头皮往前走，能多实际就多实际，给咱们俩安排出*一条*过日子的路。哦，我知道你不要我，乔治！打你还是个小不点儿起，一得着机会你就打趣我、数落我——你*就是*这样！后来，你想法子待我温和些了，可你不愿意我在眼前——哦，单是这一点我*看得*出来！你总不至于当我乐意上赶着你吧？明知道人家不要你，偏要上赶着，那滋味可不怎么好受——可我知道，不该让你孤零零一个人撂在世上；那样不好。我知道你母亲会要我照看你，给你张罗个*多少像样*的家——我知道，我尽力想做的，正是她要我做的！”这时范妮的眼泪已是苦的了，嗓音又哑又湿，字字都是悲切切的真话。“我尽力了——我尽力做到实际——照应你的利益——凡事给你张罗到我能张罗到的最好——为给咱们寻个住处，我把鞋后跟都走塌了——我在这座城里走了一遍又一遍——一个街区的电车也没坐过——再累我也不肯使那五分钱——哦！”她抽抽噎噎，再也按捺不住。“哦！可如今——你不要——你要——你要撇下我不管！你——”

===Original===
George stopped walking. “In God’s name, Aunt Fanny,” he said, “quit spreading out your handkerchief and drying it and then getting it all wet again! I mean stop crying! Do! And for heaven’s sake, get up. Don’t sit there with your back against the boiler and—”

“It’s not hot,” Fanny sniffled. “It’s cold; the plumbers disconnected it. I wouldn’t mind if they hadn’t. *I* wouldn’t mind if it burned me, George.”

“Oh, my *Lord*!” He went to her, and lifted her. “For God’s sake, get up! Come, let’s take the coffee into the other room, and see what’s to be done.”

===Chinese===
乔治停住脚。“看在上帝份上，范妮姑妈，”他说，“别再把你的手帕摊开晾一晾，回头又浸它个透湿！我是说，别哭了！千万！还有，看在老天份上，起来。别那么背靠着锅炉坐在那儿，还——”

“不烫，”范妮抽噎着。“是凉的；水管工把它卸开了。他们要是不卸才好呢。*我*就是叫它烫着也不嫌的，乔治。”

“哦，我的*老天*！”他走过去，把她搀了起来。“看在上天份上，起来！来，咱们把咖啡端到那间屋里去，看看该怎么办。”

===Original===
He got her to her feet; she leaned upon him, already somewhat comforted, and, with his arm about her, he conducted her to the dining room and seated her in one of the two kitchen chairs which had been placed at the rough table. “There!” he said, “get over it!” Then he brought the coffeepot, some lumps of sugar in a tin pan, and, finding that all the coffee-cups were broken, set water glasses upon the table, and poured some of the pale coffee into them. By this time Fanny’s spirits had revived appreciably: she looked up with a plaintive eagerness. “I *had* bought all my fall clothes, George,” she said; “and I paid every bill I owed. I don’t owe a cent for clothes, George.”

“That’s good,” he said wanly, and he had a moment of physical dizziness that decided him to sit down quickly. For an instant it seemed to him that he was not Fanny’s nephew, but married to her. He passed his pale hand over his paler forehead. “Well, let’s see where we stand,” he said feebly. “Let’s see if we can afford this place you’ve selected.”

Fanny continued to brighten. “I’m sure it’s the most practical plan we could possibly have worked out, George—and it *is* a comfort to be among nice people. I think we’ll both enjoy it, because the truth is we’ve been keeping too much to ourselves for a long while. It isn’t good for people.”

===Chinese===
他扶她站起身来；她倚着他，已经多少得了安慰；他伸臂搂着她进了饭厅，让她在摆在粗陋餐桌旁的两把厨房椅里坐下了。“好了！”他说，“缓过来吧！”随后他端来咖啡壶，还有盛在铁皮盘里的几块方糖；一瞧咖啡杯全碎了，便把玻璃水杯摆上桌，倒了些灰白的咖啡进去。这工夫，范妮的兴致已经明显回转：她抬起头，一脸哀恳的急切。“我*是*早就把秋天的衣裳置齐了，乔治，”她说；“欠人的账单也都付清了。衣裳上头，一个子儿也不欠了，乔治。”

“那敢情好，”他蔫蔫地说。跟着一阵头晕目眩袭来，叫他赶紧坐了下来。恍惚一瞬，他觉得自己仿佛不是范妮的侄子，倒像跟她成了亲。他抬起苍白的手，抹了抹更加苍白的前额。“好，咱们算算眼下是个什么光景，”他有气无力地说。“算算你选中的这地方，咱们住得住住不起。”

范妮的兴头有增无减。“我敢说，这是咱们能筹划出来的顶实际的章程了，乔治——况且同体面人住在一处，*确实*是桩宽心的事。我想咱们俩都会过得快活，因为说实话，咱们闷守自家也闷得太久了。人这么闷着没有好处。”

===Original===
“I was thinking about the money, Aunt Fanny. You see—”

“I’m sure we can manage it,” she interrupted quickly. “There really isn’t a cheaper place in town that we could actually live in and be—” Here she interrupted herself. “Oh! There’s one *great* economy I forgot to tell you, and it’s especially an economy for you, because you’re always too generous about such things: they don’t allow any tipping. They have signs that prohibit it.”

“That’s good,” he said grimly. “But the rent is thirty-six dollars a month; the dinner is twenty-two and a half for each of us, and we’ve got to have some provision for other food. We won’t need any clothes for a year, perhaps—”

===Chinese===
“我说的是钱，范妮姑妈。你瞧——”

“我担保咱们张罗得开，”她急忙截住。“城里真就再找不出一处更便宜的、咱们还能真住得下去又——”说到这里，她自己把话头截住了。“哦！有一桩*顶大*的省钱处，我忘了告诉你，这一桩尤其是为你的荷包省的，因为这种事你手总是太松：那儿一概不许给小费。还贴着告示，明令禁止。”

“那可不坏，”他冷冷地说。“可房租每月三十六块；客饭一人一顿二十二块五，别的吃食也得预备出一笔。衣服嘛，一年之内咱们兴许不必添——”

===Original===
“Oh, longer!” she exclaimed. “So you see—”

“I see that forty-five and thirty-six make eighty-one,” he said. “At the lowest, we need a hundred dollars a month—and I’m going to make thirty-two.”

“I thought of that, George,” she said confidently, “and I’m sure it will be all right. You’ll be earning a great deal more than that very soon.”

===Chinese===
“哦，还不止一年！”她嚷道。“所以你瞧——”

“我看见的是，四十五加三十六，得八十一，”他说。“打最低处算，咱们每月得要一百块——而我挣的是三十二。”

“这个我想过了，乔治，”她满有把握地说，“我保准错不了。用不了多久，你挣的就要比这多得多。”

===Original===
“I don’t see any prospect of it—not till I’m admitted to the bar, and that will be two years at the earliest.”

Fanny’s confidence was not shaken. “I know *you’ll* be getting on faster than—”

“Faster?” George echoed gravely. “We’ve got to have more than that to start with.”

===Chinese===
“我看不出半点眉目——最快也得等我考取律师资格，那起码是两年以后。”

范妮的信心分毫不动。“我知道*你*发迹会比——”

“更快？”乔治一本正经地复述道。“咱们起手就得比这更宽裕才行。”

===Original===
“Well, there’s the six hundred dollars from the sale. Six hundred and twelve dollars it was.”

“It isn’t six hundred and twelve now,” said George. “It’s about one hundred and sixty.”

Fanny showed a momentary dismay. “Why, how—”

===Chinese===
“咳，还有变卖家当的那六百块呢。原数是六百一十二块。”

“如今可不是六百一十二了，”乔治说。“只剩一百六十块上下。”

范妮一时惶然。“咦，怎么——”

===Original===
“I lent Uncle George two hundred; I gave fifty apiece to old Sam and those two other old darkies that worked for grandfather so long, and ten to each of the servants here—”

“And you gave me thirty-six,” she said thoughtfully, “for the first month’s rent, in advance.”

“Did I? I’d forgotten. Well, with about a hundred and sixty in bank and our expenses a hundred a month, it doesn’t seem as if this new place—”

===Chinese===
“我借给乔治舅舅两百；老山姆和另外两个伺候外祖父这么多年的老黑人，一人给了五十；这儿的仆人，一人给了十块——”

“你还给了我三十六，”她若有所思地说，“是头一个月的房租，预付的。”

“是吗？我倒忘了。这么着，银行里存着一百六十块上下，开销每月一百，这新住处瞧着好像——”

===Original===
“Still,” she interrupted, “we *have* paid the first month’s rent in advance, and it does seem to be the most practical—”

George rose. “See here, Aunt Fanny,” he said decisively. “You stay here and look after the moving. Old Frank doesn’t expect me until afternoon, this first day, but I’ll go and see him now.”

… It was early, and old Frank, just established at his big, flat-topped desk, was surprised when his prospective assistant and pupil walked in. He was pleased, as well as surprised, however, and rose, offering a cordial old hand. “The real flare!” he said. “The real flare for the law. That’s right! Couldn’t wait till afternoon to begin! I’m delighted that you—”

===Chinese===
“话虽如此，”她打断道，“头一个月的房租咱们*是*预付过的，况且那地方瞧着到底顶实惠——”

乔治站起身。“听着，范妮姑妈，”他斩钉截铁地说。“你留在这儿照管搬家。今天是头一天，老弗兰克原要下午才等我，可我这会儿就去见他。”

……时间还早。老弗兰克刚在大平顶书桌后安顿停当，一见他这位未来的助手兼学生跨进门来，不觉一怔。不过他又惊又喜，起身伸出一只亲热的老手。“真是天分！”他说。“学法律的真天分。这才对！连下午都等不得，就要开场！我真高兴你——”

===Original===
“I wanted to say—” George began, but his patron cut him off.

“Wait just a minute, my boy. I’ve prepared a little speech of welcome, and even though you’re five hours ahead of time, I mean to deliver it. First of all, your grandfather was my old war-comrade and my best client; for years I prospered through my connection with his business, and his grandson is welcome in my office and to my best efforts in his behalf. But I want to confess, Georgie, that during your earlier youth I may have had some slight feeling of—well, prejudice, not altogether in your favour; but whatever slight feeling it was, it began to vanish on that afternoon, a good while ago, when you stood up to your Aunt Amelia Amberson as you did in the Major’s library, and talked to her as a man and a gentleman should. I saw then what good stuff was in you—and I always wanted to mention it. If my prejudice hadn’t altogether vanished after that, the last vestiges disappeared during these trying times that have come upon you this past year, when I have been a witness to the depth of feeling you’ve shown and your quiet consideration for your grandfather and for everyone else around you. I just want to add that I think you’ll find an honest pleasure now in industry and frugality that wouldn’t have come to you in a more frivolous career. The law is a jealous mistress and a stern mistress, but a—”

George had stood before him in great and increasing embarrassment; and he was unable to allow the address to proceed to its conclusion.

===Chinese===
“我想说的是——”乔治开了个头，却被他的恩主截住。

“稍候片刻，我的孩子。我预备了一小篇欢迎辞，哪怕你足足提前了五个钟头，我也非要讲完不可。首先，你外祖父是我的战时老战友，又是我顶好的主顾；这些年我全凭同他业务上的往来兴旺发达，他的外孙进我事务所的门，欢迎之至，为他奔走，我也必尽我最大的力气。不过我得向你招认，小乔治：你早年少不更事的时候，我兴许对你存过一点小小的——怎么说呢，成见，不见得全向着你；可无论那点成见多么小，它从好些年以前那个下午就开始消散了——那天，在少校的图书室里，你就那样顶撞了你的阿米莉亚·安伯森姑妈，像男子汉、像绅士该有的那样同她讲话。那时我就看出你这块料子是块好料——只是始终想提上一句。那以后我的成见若还没有消尽，那么最后一点残余，也在过去这一年临到你头上的艰难时日里消磨干净了——你所显露的情意何等深挚，你又何等不动声色地体恤你的外祖父和身边的每一个人，我都亲眼看着。我只想再添一句：我想你会发现，如今在勤勉与俭朴之中，你自会尝到一种正当的乐趣，这乐趣，换一条轻浮些的营生之路是到不了你身上的。法律是一位善妒的情妇，一位严厉的情妇，可——”

乔治一直站在他跟前，窘迫之色有增无已，实在由不得这篇颂词讲到结尾。

===Original===
“I can’t do it!” he burst out. “I can’t take her for my mistress.”

“What?”

“I’ve come to tell you, I’ve got to find something that’s quicker. I can’t—”

===Chinese===
“我干不了这个！”他脱口喊道。“我不能让她做我的情妇。”

“什么？”

“我就是来告诉你的，我得寻一样来钱更快的营生。我——”

===Original===
Old Frank got a little red. “Let’s sit down,” he said. “What’s the trouble?”

George told him.

The old gentleman listened sympathetically, only murmuring: “Well, well!” from time to time, and nodding acquiescence.

===Chinese===
老弗兰克微微红了脸。“咱们坐下说，”他说。“到底什么难处？”

乔治照实说了。

老先生满怀同情地听着，只是不时低声念叨一句：“哦，哦！”一面颔首应许。

===Original===
“You see she’s set her mind on this apartment,” George explained. “She’s got some old cronies there, and I guess she’s been looking forward to the games of bridge and the kind of harmless gossip that goes on in such places. Really, it’s a life she’d like better than anything else—better than that she’s lived at home, I really believe. It struck me she’s just about got to have it, and after all she could hardly have anything less.”

“This comes pretty heavily upon me, you know,” said old Frank. “I got her into that headlight company, and she fooled me about her resources as much as she did your Uncle George. I was never your father’s adviser, if you remember, and when the insurance was turned over to her some other lawyer arranged it—probably your father’s. But it comes pretty heavily on me, and I feel a certain responsibility.”

“Not at all. I’m taking the responsibility.” And George smiled with one corner of his mouth. “She’s not *your* aunt, you know, sir.”

===Chinese===
“您瞧，她一门心思全拴在这套公寓上了，”乔治解释道。“那儿有她几个老相识，我猜她早就眼巴巴盼着那儿的桥牌局，盼着那类地方才有的那种无害的闲磕牙。说真的，那样的日子，她比什么都中意——比她在家里过的日子中意，这一层我是真信。我猛然省悟过来，这日子她几乎是少不得的；况且说到底，比这再单薄的，她也简直无从指望了。”

“你要知道，这在我这边担子着实不轻，”老弗兰克说。“头灯公司是我引她进去的；她在自家财产底细上瞒我的工夫，不输瞒你乔治舅舅半分。你该记得，我一向不是你父亲的顾问，那份保险金转到她名下，是别的律师经的手——多半是你父亲请的。可这桩事压在我心上沉甸甸的，我觉得自己也有一份干系。”

“哪里的话。这份干系我来担。”乔治嘴角挑起一丝笑。“要知道，先生，她可不是*您*的姑妈。”

===Original===
“Well, I’m unable to see, even if she’s yours, that a young man is morally called upon to give up a career at the law to provide his aunt with a favourable opportunity to play bridge whist!”

“No,” George agreed. “But I haven’t begun my ‘career at the law’ so it can’t be said I’m making any considerable sacrifice. I’ll tell you how it is, sir.” He flushed, and, looking out of the streaked and smoky window beside which he was sitting, spoke with difficulty. “I feel as if—as if perhaps I had one or two pretty important things in my life to make up for. Well, I can’t. I can’t make them up to—to whom I would. It’s struck me that, as I couldn’t, I might be a little decent to somebody else, perhaps—if I could manage it! I never have been particularly decent to poor old Aunt Fanny.”

“Oh, I don’t know: I shouldn’t say that. A little youthful teasing—I doubt if she’s minded so much. She felt your father’s death terrifically, of course, but it seems to me she’s had a fairly comfortable life—up to now—if she was disposed to take it that way.”

===Chinese===
“哼，就算她是你的姑妈，我也看不出天理人情哪一条规定，一个年轻人就该放弃法律生涯，好给他姑妈腾出一个打桥牌惠斯特的良机！”

“这话不假，”乔治应道。“可我的‘法律生涯’还没开场，谈不上牺牲了什么了不得的东西。先生，我跟您交个底。”他红了脸，望着自己坐处旁边那扇沾满烟痕水渍的窗子，说得很艰难。“我觉得，仿佛——仿佛我这一生里，兴许有那么一两件顶要紧的事，等着我去补偿。咳，我补偿不了。我没法补偿给——给我想补偿的那个人。于是我想，既然补偿不成，兴许我可以多少厚道地待一待旁人——只要我办得到！可怜的老范妮姑妈，我待她从来谈不上怎么厚道。”

“哦，这我可不敢附和：我不觉得有那么糟。小孩子家的一点逗弄——我看她未必怎么往心里去。你父亲去世，她当然哀恸欲绝；可要依我看，她这日子过得一向还算安稳——直到如今——只要她自己肯那么看。”

===Original===
“But ‘up to now’ is the important thing,” George said. “Now is now—and you see I can’t wait two years to be admitted to the bar and begin to practice. I’ve got to start in at something else that pays from the start, and that’s what I’ve come to you about. I have an idea, you see.”

“Well, I’m glad of that!” said old Frank, smiling. “I can’t think of anything just at this minute that pays from the start.”

“I only know of one thing, myself.”

===Chinese===
“可‘直到如今’才是要紧处，”乔治说。“如今就是如今——您瞧，我等不得两年去考律师资格、再去开业。我得从一样打头一天就见钱的行当干起，我正是为这个来求您的。您瞧，我有个主意。”

“哦，有主意就好！”老弗兰克微笑道。“只是这会儿，打头一天就见钱的行当，我一样也想不出来。”

“我自己统共知道一样。”

===Original===
“What is it?”

George flushed again, but managed to laugh at his own embarrassment. “I suppose I’m about as ignorant of business as anybody in the world,” he said. “But I’ve heard they pay very high wages to people in dangerous trades; I’ve always heard they did, and I’m sure it must be true. I mean people that handle touchy chemicals or high explosives—men in dynamite factories, or who take things of that sort about the country in wagons, and shoot oil wells. I thought I’d see if you couldn’t tell me something more about it, or else introduce me to someone who could, and then I thought I’d see if I couldn’t get something of the kind to do as soon as possible. My nerves are good; I’m muscular, and I’ve got a steady hand; it seemed to me that this was about the only line of work in the world that I’m fitted for. I wanted to get started today if I could.”

Old Frank gave him a long stare. At first this scrutiny was sharply incredulous; then it was grave; finally it developed into a threat of overwhelming laughter; a forked vein in his forehead became more visible and his eyes seemed about to protrude.

But he controlled his impulse; and, rising, took up his hat and overcoat. “All right,” he said. “If you’ll promise not to get blown up, I’ll go with you to see if we can find the job.” Then, meaning what he said, but amazed that he did mean it, he added: “You certainly are the most practical young man I ever met!”

===Chinese===
“什么行当？”

乔治又红了脸，却竭力对着自己的窘态笑了一笑。“我想，讲起做买卖，我在世上算数一数二的无知了，”他说。“可我听说，干危险行当的人，工钱出得极高；我一向这么听说，谅来不会是假。我是说那些摆弄性烈的化学品或烈性炸药的人——火药厂里的工人，或是赶着大车把那类东西运往乡间、给油井放炮的人。我想先请您给我多讲一讲这中间的门道，要不就引荐一位懂行的人给我；随后我想，看我能不能尽快寻摸着一份这样的差事。我的神经顶稳；筋骨结实，手上也稳；在我看来，普天之下，这差不多是唯一一桩我干得了的行当。只要办得到，我今天就想上手。”

老弗兰克久久地盯着他。这番端详起先是尖利的、满脸的不肯置信；继而转为凝重；末了竟酿成一场大笑的先兆，眼看要决堤而出；他额头上一根分叉的青筋愈发分明，眼珠也仿佛要迸出眶来。

可他按住了这股冲动，起身拿起帽子和大衣。“好，”他说。“只要你答应不叫自己炸上天，我就陪你走一趟，看能不能寻着这份差事。”随后他又找补一句——这话他本是当真的，只是连他自己也惊异自己竟当了真：“你实在是我这辈子遇见过的顶实际的一位年轻人！”

## XXXIII / 三十三

===Original===
They found the job. It needed an apprenticeship of only six weeks, during which period George was to receive fifteen dollars a week; after that he would get twenty-eight. This settled the apartment question, and Fanny was presently established in a greater contentment than she had known for a long time. Early every morning she made something she called (and believed to be) coffee for George, and he was gallant enough not to undeceive her. She lunched alone in her “kitchenette,” for George’s place of employment was ten miles out of town on an interurban trolley-line, and he seldom returned before seven. Fanny found partners for bridge by two o’clock almost every afternoon, and she played until about six. Then she got George’s “dinner clothes” out for him—he maintained this habit—and she changed her own dress. When he arrived he usually denied that he was tired, though he sometimes looked tired, particularly during the first few months; and he explained to her frequently—looking bored enough with her insistence—that his work was “fairly light, and fairly congenial, too.” Fanny had the foggiest idea of what it was, though she noticed that it roughened his hands and stained them. “Something in those new chemical works,” she explained to casual inquirers. It was not more definite in her own mind.

Respect for George undoubtedly increased within her, however, and she told him she’d always had a feeling he might “turn out to be a mechanical genius, or something.” George assented with a nod, as the easiest course open to him. He did not take a hand at bridge after dinner: his provisions for Fanny’s happiness refused to extend that far, and at the table-d’hôte he was a rather discouraging boarder. He was considered “affected” and absurdly “up-stage” by the one or two young men, and the three or four young women, who enlivened the elderly retreat; and was possibly less popular there than he had been elsewhere during his life, though he was now nothing worse than a coldly polite young man who kept to himself. After dinner he would escort his aunt from the table in some state (not wholly unaccompanied by a leerish wink or two from the wags of the place) and he would leave her at the door of the communal parlours and card rooms, with a formality in his bow of farewell which afforded an amusing contrast to Fanny’s always voluble protests. (She never failed to urge loudly that he really *must* come and play, just this once, and not go hiding from everybody in his room *every* evening like this!) At least some of the other inhabitants found the contrast amusing, for sometimes, as he departed stiffly toward the elevator, leaving her still entreating in the doorway (though with one eye already on her table, to see that it was not seized) a titter would follow him which he was no doubt meant to hear. He did not care whether they laughed or not.

And once, as he passed the one or two young men of the place entertaining the three or four young women, who were elbowing and jerking on a settee in the lobby, he heard a voice inquiring quickly, as he passed:

===Chinese===
活儿总算寻着了。这份活儿只消当六个星期的学徒，学徒期间乔治每周领十五美元；期满之后，就能拿二十八。这一来，住公寓的问题便解决了，不多时，范妮就安顿了下来，那份称心如意，是她许久以来不曾有过的。每天一早，她给乔治弄一样她称之为（并且深信不疑是）咖啡的东西，而乔治颇有骑士风度，没有去戳穿她。她独自一人在自己那间“小厨房”里用午饭，因为乔治上班的地方在城外十英里，通的是城际电车线，他难得在七点以前回来。几乎每天下午两点来钟，范妮就约齐了桥牌搭子，一路打到六点上下。然后她替乔治把“赴晚宴的衣裳”取出来——这习惯他始终没丢——自己也换了衣裳。他到家时往往不肯认账，说不累，虽然有时看着是真累，头几个月尤其如此；她一追问，他便常常作解释——一脸被缠得腻烦透了的神气——说他的活儿“相当轻省，也相当合意”。这活儿究竟是干什么的，范妮只有一星半点的模糊影子，只是她留意到它磨粗了他的手，还把手染污了。“在新开的那家化工厂里做点事，”遇上有人随口问起，她便这样答。她自己心里所知道的，也并不比这句更明确。

不过，她对乔治的敬重确乎是与日俱增，还跟他说，她一向就有个预感，觉得他保不齐“到底是个机械天才，或者别的什么才”。乔治点头应承，这是他眼下最省事的一条路。晚饭后他不上牌桌打桥牌：他为范妮的幸福备下的种种安排，断不肯周全到这一地步；而在包桌的饭席上，他也是个相当败人兴致的搭伙客。在给这处老年退居之所平添生气的那一两位小伙子、三四位姑娘看来，他“做作”，又“拿大”得可笑；他在这里的人缘，兴许比他这辈子在任何地方都差，虽然如今的他，充其量不过是个待人冷淡客气、独来独往的年轻人罢了。晚饭后，他总要在几分排场之中护送姑妈离席（那地方的促狭鬼们免不了要冲他们斜睨挤眼，一下两下是少不了的），把她送到公用客厅和牌室门口，告别的一躬礼数十足，同范妮一贯滔滔不绝的推拒恰成一面有趣的镜子。（她断没有一回不高声相劝：他*务必*来玩一把，就这一回，可别*天天*晚上这样躲进屋去，谁也不见！）至少别的一些住客觉得这面镜子有趣，因为有时，他僵硬地朝电梯走去，把她还留在门口苦劝不放（不过她一只眼早已瞟住自己的牌桌，看它别叫人占了去），身后便跟来一阵窃笑——那笑分明是有意让他听见的。人家笑与不笑，他全不在乎。

有一回，那地方的一两位小伙子正陪着三四位姑娘取乐，姑娘们在门厅的长椅上推推搡搡、扭来扭去；他打旁边走过时，听见一个声音抢着问道：

===Original===
“What makes people tired?”

“Work?”

“No.”

“Well, what’s the answer?”

Then, with an intentional outbreak of mirth, the answer was given by two loudly whispering voices together:

“A stuck-up boarder!”

George didn’t care.

===Chinese===
“什么最能叫人累？”

“干活儿？”

“不是。”

“那你说答案是什么？”

随后，一阵故意发作出来的哄笑里，两个扯高了嗓门交头接耳的声音，一齐给出了答案：

“一个摆臭架子的搭伙客！”

乔治不在乎。

===Original===
On Sunday mornings Fanny went to church and George took long walks. He explored the new city, and found it hideous, especially in the early spring, before the leaves of the shade trees were out. Then the town was fagged with the long winter and blacked with the heavier smoke that had been held close to the earth by the smoke-fog it bred. Everything was damply streaked with the soot: the walls of the houses, inside and out, the gray curtains at the windows, the windows themselves, the dirty cement and unswept asphalt underfoot, the very sky overhead. Throughout this murky season he continued his explorations, never seeing a face he knew—for, on Sunday, those whom he remembered, or who might remember him, were not apt to be found within the limits of the town, but were congenially occupied with the new outdoor life which had come to be the mode since his boyhood. He and Fanny were pretty thoroughly buried away within the bigness of the city.

One of his Sunday walks, that spring, he made into a sour pilgrimage. It was a misty morning of belated snow slush, and suited him to a perfection of miserableness, as he stood before the great dripping department store which now occupied the big plot of ground where once had stood both the Amberson Hotel and the Amberson Opera House. From there he drifted to the old “Amberson Block,” but this was fallen into a backwater; business had stagnated here. The old structure had not been replaced, but a cavernous entryway for trucks had been torn in its front, and upon the cornice, where the old separate metal letters had spelt “Amberson Block,” there was a long billboard sign: “Doogan Storage.”

===Chinese===
星期天上午，范妮上教堂，乔治则作长距离的散步。他把这座长成了的新城探了个遍，只觉得它丑陋不堪，早春时节尤其如此——那时行道树的叶子还没有舒展。整个城被漫长的冬天拖得疲惫委顿，又被浓得多的煤烟抹得乌黑——那烟本被它自己生出的烟霭按得紧贴地面，散不出去。样样东西都让烟灰洇出一道道湿漉漉的黑痕：房屋的墙壁，里里外外；窗口的灰窗帘；窗户本身；脚下的洋灰路面脏兮兮的，沥青街面无人清扫；连头顶上的天也未能幸免。整个阴晦的季节，他探访不辍，却从未遇见一张认得的脸——因为逢了星期天，他记得的、或是可能记得他的人们，多半不在城区之内，而是各自陶然地忙着过新的户外生活——自他少年时代以来，那已成了时兴。他和范妮，就这样埋进了这座大城的浩大之中，埋得相当彻底。

那年春天，他有一个星期天的散步，是走成了一趟苦味的朝圣。那是个雾蒙蒙的早晨，迟来的雪化成了烂雪泥，同他这份凄惶的心境配合得严丝合缝。他站在一座滴滴答答淌着水的大百货公司跟前——如今占着这片大空地的正是它，而当年，安伯森旅馆和安伯森歌剧院一同立在这块地上。从那里他又荡到旧日的“安伯森大楼”，可此地已退成一泓死水，买卖都停滞了。旧楼没有拆去重建，只是正面被凿开一个岩洞似的豁口，好走货车；当年一颗颗分嵌的金属字母拼出“安伯森大楼”字样的那道檐口上，如今横着一条长长的广告牌：“杜根仓储行。”

===Original===
To spare himself nothing, he went out National Avenue and saw the piles of slush-covered wreckage where the Mansion and his mother’s house had been, and where the Major’s ill-fated five “new” houses had stood; for these were down, too, to make room for the great tenement already shaped in unending lines of foundation. But the Fountain of Neptune was gone at last—and George was glad that it was!

He turned away from the devastated site, thinking bitterly that the only Amberson mark still left upon the town was the name of the boulevard—Amberson Boulevard. But he had reckoned without the city council of the new order, and by an unpleasant coincidence, while the thought was still in his mind, his eye fell upon a metal oblong sign upon the lamppost at the corner. There were two of these little signs upon the lamppost, at an obtuse angle to each other, one to give passersby the name of National Avenue, the other to acquaint them with Amberson Boulevard. But the one upon which should have been stenciled “Amberson Boulevard” exhibited the words “Tenth Street.”

George stared at it hard. Then he walked quickly along the boulevard to the next corner and looked at the little sign there. “Tenth Street.”

It had begun to rain, but George stood unheeding, staring at the little sign. “Damn them!” he said finally, and, turning up his coat-collar, plodded back through the soggy streets toward “home.”

===Chinese===
他不给自己留半点余地，沿着国民大道一路出城，只见公馆和他母亲旧宅的原址上，蒙着雪泥的残骸堆成了小山；少校那五座福薄命乖的“新”房子立过的地方也一样——它们也拆平了，好腾地方给一座大杂院式的巨构公寓，那一行接一行望不到头的地基，已经勾出了它的身形。只是尼普顿喷泉终究是没有了——乔治高兴它没有！

他从这片劫后的废址上转过身去，心里发着酸苦：这座城身上剩下的安伯森印记，如今只有那大道的名字了——安伯森大道。可他没有把新秩序的市议会算在里头；偏偏又巧得叫人心里发堵，这念头还没转完，他的目光就落在街角灯杆上一块长方形的金属小牌上。灯杆上钉着两块这样的小牌，彼此斜成一个钝角：一块告诉路人这里是国民大道，另一块本该告诉他们这里是安伯森大道。可那块本该模印着“安伯森大道”的，赫然写的是“第十街”。

乔治狠狠地瞪着它。随后他顺着大道快步走到下一个街角，看那里的小牌。“第十街。”

雨已经下起来，乔治却浑然不觉，兀自瞪着那块小牌。“该死的家伙们！”他终于开了口，随即将大衣领子竖起，拖着沉沉的步子，踩着一街泥泞，往“家”走回去。

===Original===
The utilitarian impudence of the city authorities put a thought into his mind. A week earlier he had happened to stroll into the large parlour of the apartment house, finding it empty, and on the center table he noticed a large, red-bound, gilt-edged book, newly printed, bearing the title: “A Civic History,” and beneath the title, the rubric, “Biographies of the 500 Most Prominent Citizens and Families in the History of the City.” He had glanced at it absently, merely noticing the title and subtitle, and wandered out of the room, thinking of other things and feeling no curiosity about the book. But he had thought of it several times since with a faint, vague uneasiness; and now when he entered the lobby he walked directly into the parlour where he had seen the book. The room was empty, as it always was on Sunday mornings, and the flamboyant volume was still upon the table—evidently a fixture as a sort of local *Almanach de Gotha*, or Burke, for the enlightenment of tenants and boarders.

He opened it, finding a few painful steel engravings of placid, chin-bearded faces, some of which he remembered dimly; but much more numerous, and also more unfamiliar to him, were the pictures of neat, aggressive men, with clipped short hair and clipped short moustaches—almost all of them strangers to him. He delayed not long with these, but turned to the index where the names of the five hundred Most Prominent Citizens and Families in the History of the City were arranged in alphabetical order, and ran his finger down the column of *A*’s:

===Chinese===
市政当局这副实利主义的厚脸皮，往他心里搁进了一个念头。一个星期之前，他碰巧踱进公寓的大客厅，见四下无人；屋当中一张桌上，摆着一部新印的大书，红封皮，洒金边，题着书名：《市政史》；题名底下另有一行小字：《本城历史上五百位最显要的市民与家族列传》。他当时心不在焉地扫了一眼，只记得正题副题，便信步出了屋，满脑子别的心事，对那部书没有半分好奇。可打那以后，它几次三番浮上心头，带着一缕隐隐约约的不安；这会儿进了门厅，他便径直走进先前见着那部书的客厅。屋里空空如也，星期天上午它向来如此；那部花团锦簇的大书仍旧端踞桌上——分明是一件常设的陈设，一部本地的《哥达年鉴》（*Almanach de Gotha*），或是《伯克名鉴》（Burke），专供开导房客与搭伙客之用。

他翻开书，只见几幅刻得艰涩的钢版像，尽是些神情安详、颏下蓄须的面孔，其中几张他还依稀记得；可数量多得多、也生分得多的一批，是那些干净利落、咄咄逼人的男人的画像：短发齐茬，短髭齐整——几乎张张都是素昧平生。他没有在这些画像上多作流连，径直翻到索引——本城历史上五百位最显要的市民与家族按字母次序排在那里——手指顺着 *A* 字头一栏往下捋：

===Original===
Abbett

Adams

Abbott

Adams

Abrams

Adler

Akers

Andrews

Albertsmeyer

Appenbasch

Alexander

Archer

Allen

Arszman

Ambrose

Ashcraft

Ambuhl

Austin

Anderson

Avey

===Chinese===
阿贝特

亚当斯

阿博特

亚当斯

艾布拉姆斯

阿德勒

艾克斯

安德鲁斯

阿尔伯茨迈耶

阿彭巴赫

亚历山大

奥斯汀

安德森
